AQ Group · AQ-SE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 25 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the AQ Group memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on AQ Group was written: 50,249 words, against 11,374 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 24,924 words · written 25 October 2026, 11:05 to 11:25 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
AQ Group (AQ-SE): answers to the first set of questions

Written 2026-10-25 for the Investment Analyst who builds the operating and financial model. Money in SEK; "calculated" marks a figure I derived from the files rather than one stated in them. Three framing facts run through every answer and are stated once here so the reader can hold them throughout:

  1. AQ is two businesses inside one set of accounts. A large cyclical base (roughly 85 to 88 percent of sales: wiring harnesses for trucks and buses, sheet metal, injection moulding, system assembly, conventional inductive components) that grows low-to-mid single digits organically through a cycle at an EBIT margin of about 8 to 9 percent; and a small structural slice (roughly 12 to 15 percent of sales: medium-voltage transformers and inductors for data centres at about 6 percent of group sales in Q2 2026, defence at about 5 to 7 percent, grid) growing at 30 to 60 percent with, on every qualitative account, a higher margin (segment margins are not disclosed). Almost every judgement below is a judgement about how fast the slice grows, what margin it carries, and how the base behaves underneath it.
  2. The latest data point is exceptional and partly cyclical. Q3 2026 (reported 15 October 2026): revenue SEK 2,566m, up 21.4 percent; organic growth 17.9 percent (Q1 6.3, Q2 9.1); EBIT SEK 259m, up 36 percent, margin 10.1 percent; EBT margin 10.0 percent; EBIT 16.7 percent above DNB Carnegie's forecast; operating cash flow SEK 269m (Q2 SEK 23m). It was struck against a trough comparison (organic minus 9 percent in Q4 2024, 2.0 percent for FY2025) and in a quarter when the global manufacturing PMI reached about 53 and the sector digest describes an electronics restocking pulse expected to plateau around Q4 2026. The modeller should not extrapolate 17.9 percent.
  3. The share price has de-rated without a fundamental break. The price fell from SEK 248.0 (6 October, the memo's reference) to SEK 226.6 (23 October) while FY2027 consensus EPS rose 8 percent in a month; the 24-month-forward P/E fell from the 91st to the 49th percentile of its five-year history (20.83x). The fall coincides with a risk-off regime in which the AI-infrastructure complex is being sold on its financing, which is AQ's growth narrative.

External change forces

C6710 questions
C-0174C-0255C-0363C-0457C-0542C-0676C-0745C-0878C-0955C-1068

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external environment is the most supportive for AQ's earnings in at least four years, and the support is structural rather than merely cyclical: AI data-centre power, European defence and grid spending are sector-level forces with two-plus years of visibility acting on AQ's design-responsible, higher-margin products, layered on a cyclical base that has stopped contracting. The evidence is not only narrative: organic growth went from minus 6.9 percent (FY2024) to 17.9 percent (Q3 2026) on volume, not price, with data-centre inductive components up more than 60 percent.

The judgement the Consumer most needs is about proportion and timing. The structural slice is still only about 12 to 15 percent of sales; the remaining 85 to 88 percent faces an ordinary European industrial cycle that is flat-to-improving but fragile under 5 percent bond yields and weak German growth. Q3 2026 was flattered by easy comparisons, restocking and catch-up; the rate of organic growth is likely near its peak and should decelerate through 2027 toward 8 to 10 percent as comparisons harden and capacity binds, even while the level of demand keeps rising. The external headwinds that do bite (copper and steel lags, Eastern European wages, Pillar II from 2027) are mostly timing or dated steps that a model can carry explicitly. The headwinds that matter most for the investment case are external to the P&L: the discount rate and the AI-financing scare are compressing the multiple of exactly this kind of stock, and the 800V DC/solid-state transformer transition in 2027-2030 will decide whether the data-centre line keeps compounding or plateaus.

C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility?

C74

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Judgement: demand is improving on both axes at once, and the structural part has genuine two-plus-year visibility while the cyclical part has six to twelve months at best. This is the most favourable demand configuration AQ has had since the 2022-23 price-led boom, and it is better in quality because it is volume-led and concentrated in design-responsible products.

  • Structural, high visibility (two to four years): (a) AI data-centre power. Inductive components for electrification including data centres grew more than 60 percent year on year in Q3 2026; all data-centre factories run "at full throttle"; output of medium-voltage UPS transformers doubled sequentially in Q2 to 70 systems a month; DNB Carnegie says demand is "developing faster than management expected"; the sector digest expects rising rack power to lift inductor content to about 2030, with content per megawatt rising from about USD 2.5m to above USD 3.25m for low-voltage DC and about USD 3.8m with solid-state transformers. (b) European defence: SEK 60m capacity in northern Sweden, UK defence sales doubled year on year (Q1 2026), several large RFPs with nominations expected late 2026; budgets are multi-year and government-anchored. (c) Grid: Svenska kraftnät (EUR 20bn) and Fingrid (EUR 5bn) over 2026-35; grid projects regaining momentum after a weak H1 2026.
  • Cyclical, low visibility (one to three quarters): heavy trucks and buses (European truck orders falling sequentially; Mexico and Plattsburgh wiring plants underutilised since 2025 on lost bus volumes), agricultural machinery, marine and food-packaging equipment weak; construction equipment in Europe resilient; global PMI about 53 with capex broadening (global industrial capex ex-technology plus 3 percent in 2027 per Rothschild & Co Redburn); Danske Bank sees heavy trucks in an early upturn. Order books are short (Inderes), so AQ has little backlog visibility on the base.
  • The magnitude matters more than the direction. The structural slice is about 12 to 15 percent of sales. Growing at 40 percent it adds about 5 to 6 points to group organic growth (calculated); the base at plus 3 percent adds about 2.5 points. That arithmetic is why group organic growth of 8 to 10 percent is a plausible through-cycle run-rate for 2027 and why 17.9 percent is not: Q3 must also have carried broad-based cyclical strength (US and Asia demand, restocking, easy comparisons) that the sector digest expects to fade.
  • Skew and cone: the cone for the structural slice is wide on timing (US data-centre permitting pause in Texas, September 2026; defence equipment delays in Finland in Q3) but narrow on direction through 2028. The base's cone is symmetric and depends on European industrial production.

For the model: treat data-centre and defence as a separately driven line growing 30 to 50 percent in 2027 decelerating toward 15 to 25 percent in 2028 as capacity, not demand, binds; treat the base as plus 2 to 4 percent organic with a downside case of minus 5 to minus 7 percent (the FY2024 precedent).

C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C55

Judgement: competitive intensity is broadly stable overall, rising at the low-cost and commoditised edges, and falling in AQ's growth niche where demand exceeds qualified capacity. The net effect on AQ in the next six months is neutral to slightly positive; over two to three years it is negative as capacity arrives.

  • Falling intensity where AQ is growing: in US medium-voltage transformers and inductors DNB Carnegie reports AQ "winning every US quote submitted"; Virginia is fully utilised at its USD 50m capacity; Morgan Stanley notes equipment lead times for new transformer capacity exceeding six years in some cases, which protects incumbents. The sector digest's frame applies: the scarce thing is a qualified design slot, not floor space.
  • Rising intensity at the edges: Incap's EBITA margin fell to 9.0 percent in H1 2026 from a 13.4 percent median on Indian price competition; Inderes notes existing customers are also squeezing contract manufacturers on renewals; Scanfil has set a 15 percent growth target and is adding capacity aimed at electrification and medtech; Tamura is expanding in Mexico; Kitron has taken defence share (revenue up 71.7 percent in Q2 2026).
  • Structural constant: OEM customers demand annual productivity price cuts of 3 to 5 percent and dual-source; that pressure does not change with the cycle but bites harder when volumes fall.
  • Peer read: the peer files show the same pattern in adjacent component industries: Bekaert faces Chinese tyre-cord overcapacity and Asian price-volume strategies; SFS and Comer keep double-digit margins only in custom, co-engineered parts. Intensity is lowest where the part is designed in.

The direction to watch is the capacity cycle in data-centre magnetics: every competitor that adds US capacity shortens AQ's "winning every quote" window. That window is a capacity cycle, not a moat (the Wisdom Library's "early-mover window is not a moat").

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

C63

Judgement: the dominant disruption force (AI-driven electrification of data centres and grids) is strengthening and acts in AQ's favour now; its next phase (800V DC and solid-state transformers, 2027-2030) is two-sided and is the single largest medium-term technology risk to the growth slice.

  • Strengthening, positive now: AI compute is shifting power architecture toward medium-voltage UPS and, next, high-voltage DC. AQ supplies transformers, inductors and sheet-metal enclosures into ABB's HiPerGuard medium-voltage UPS (deployed at scale in North American AI data centres, including Applied Digital's Polaris Forge 2). Gas-turbine parts demand is also rising as data centres seek on-site power, and HVAC inductive components grew 30 percent organically in Shanghai on cooling demand.
  • Two-sided, 2027-2030: ABB management projects 800V DC could be 40 to 50 percent of the AI data-centre market by 2030, with meaningful orders from 2028. Deutsche Bank warns value may migrate to large electrical vendors with grid-to-rack breadth and that smaller component suppliers may struggle to keep pace with multi-system reference designs. The sector digest names 2026-2028 as the window in which designs are re-awarded. AQ's mitigation is real but early: it has delivered inductive prototypes for a solid-state transformer to a leading electrification customer, with commercial production expected 2027-2028, and is working on designs with a leading chip-maker.
  • Automation as a cost disruption, positive: robotic welding, 24-hour robot-tended injection moulding, a new powder-coating line in Bulgaria; manual winding in transformers remains hard to automate, which keeps labour cost and skilled-labour scarcity in play.
  • Disproved or weakening: none of the threats to AQ's base (insourcing by OEMs, substitution of wiring harnesses) is strengthening; commercial-vehicle electrification creates new harness and battery-enclosure demand (new e-truck harness nomination in Q3 2026).

The modeller should carry the 800V DC transition as a binary-ish risk to the data-centre line after 2028 (either AQ is designed in and the content per megawatt rises, or the line plateaus) rather than as a smooth fade; there is no evidence yet to choose between the branches.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

C57

Judgement: substantial change is under way and its net effect is mildly positive on demand and mildly negative on cost and tax; the one change with a clear, dated, quantifiable effect on EPS is negative (OECD Pillar II from 2027).

  • Positive (demand): European rearmament budgets; Nordic grid programmes (EUR 25bn combined over 2026-35); nearshoring and domestic-sourcing rules (a former Northrop Grumman consultant cites DFAR-type requirements forcing defence primes toward tariff-insulated domestic suppliers); US tariffs have had negligible direct impact because AQ makes locally (17 countries), and the USD 3.7m Wytheville expansion and Norlake put US transformer output inside the tariff wall.
  • Negative (cost and complexity): the EU Steel Overcapacity Regulation doubled out-of-quota duties to 50 percent from 1 July 2026 with melt-origin documentation from 1 October 2026 (steel up 36.7 percent year on year); CSRD and CBAM compliance (the CEO calls EU regulation a constant tax on productivity); a Texas pause on new data-centre permitting (September 2026), which Jefferies expects to lift after the November midterms; about 6 percent of 2025 sales went to US customers and Mexico and Canada sites face trade-barrier risk.
  • Negative (tax, dated): AQ reports under Pillar II from 1 January 2025 and the CFO expects the first additional tax payments in 2027, because significant profits arise in low-tax Bulgaria, Estonia and Lithuania. The reported tax rate was 18.1 percent in FY2025; consensus carries about 20 percent for FY2026-28 (calculated from consensus pre-tax and net income); the memo's path rises to about 22 percent by FY2028. Each point of tax is about 1.3 percent of EPS. This is the most model-relevant regulatory item.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

❌ D42

Judgement: a moderate, timing-driven headwind. Input prices are rising sharply (copper at records, steel up, electronic components up, Eastern European wages up), availability is adequate, and supplier concentration is low. Because AQ passes metal prices through contractually with a 3-to-6-month lag and holds no commodity or currency hedges, the effect is a temporary margin dip after each spike, not a permanent loss; the permanent pressure is labour inflation and OEM productivity demands.

  • Metals: copper above USD 14,000 a tonne in May 2026, about USD 6.63 a pound in August; Goldman Sachs' 2027 LME forecast USD 13,800; copper winding wire is the core input to the inductive business. Steel up 36.7 percent year on year at a three-year high. AQ buys semi-finished goods (sheet, insulated wire, cable), not raw metal, so its exposure is indirect but real.
  • The lag is proven in the history: in FY2022 revenue rose SEK 1,582m but EBIT only SEK 58m (incremental margin 3.7 percent, calculated) as inflation outran clauses; in FY2023 revenue rose SEK 1,915m and EBIT SEK 342m (incremental 17.9 percent, calculated) as prices caught up. The same lag is visible in peers (Comer and SFS pass through with a 3-to-6-month lag; Tokyo Rope and Rajratan cite the same).
  • A mechanical margin-percentage effect: pass-through of a higher copper price inflates revenue at zero margin, so the EBIT margin percentage falls even when EBIT in SEK is protected. Consensus value-added gross margin (gross profit over revenue on the brokers' basis) of about 51 percent for FY2026 against 53.8 percent in 2025 is consistent with this. The modeller should separate metal-price pass-through from volume when judging margin.
  • Labour: about 65 percent of staff are in Bulgaria, Poland, Lithuania and Estonia, where minimum-wage rises run ahead of productivity; staff turnover was 20 percent in 2025.
  • Electronics: Ericsson warns of AI-driven component price rises; the System segment absorbs these through semi-finished purchases.
  • Supplier concentration: low; dual-sourcing policy; no suppliers in Russia, Ukraine, Belarus or the Middle East; local sourcing.
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

B76

Judgement: the cycle has turned decisively and is in an early-to-mid upswing in level, but the rate of change is probably at or near its peak in Q3-Q4 2026. The turn is real, volume-led and broad (not only data centres), and it began from a genuine trough.

  • The sequence: organic growth 20.7 percent (FY2023, price-led), minus 9 percent (Q4 2024), minus 6.9 percent (FY2024), 2.0 percent (FY2025), then 6.3, 9.1 and 17.9 percent in Q1-Q3 2026. Revenue was SEK 7,500m for nine months, up 11.1 percent. Two-year stacked revenue growth in Q3 2026 was 31.7 percent (2,566 against 1,949 in Q3 2024, calculated, including acquisitions and currency).
  • Consensus already embeds a further step: FY2026 consensus revenue of SEK 10,226m implies Q4 2026 revenue of about SEK 2,726m, up about 17 percent on Q4 2025 (calculated), helped by backlog catch-up in data centres and Finnish defence (equipment installed in October 2026) and by Norlake's first full quarter.
  • Why the rate of change likely peaks now: comparisons get harder from Q1 2027 (Q1 2026 was the first quarter of recovery); the electronics restocking pulse is expected to plateau around Q4 2026; data-centre growth will be bounded by capacity (all sites full; Norlake and 2027 additions are the next capacity steps). Consensus revenue growth of 11.2 percent for FY2027 and 7.5 percent for FY2028 already assumes deceleration.
  • Currency, a hidden support to reported growth: the krona drag that cost minus 4.8 percent in Q1 2026 was minus 0.1 percent in Q2 and about plus 1.3 percent in Q3 (calculated as the residual of 21.4 less 17.9 organic less 2.2 acquired). The reported-growth tailwind from a fading drag is ending; Morgan Stanley is bullish on the krona, which would turn it back into a headwind in 2027.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

❌ D45

Judgement: macro forces are acting strongly, and they bite hardest at the valuation (sector and market) level, second on the cyclical base of the business, and least on the structural slice. For an 18-month model the macro risk is mostly a multiple and sentiment risk, not an earnings risk, unless Europe enters recession.

  • Discount rate and risk appetite (biting now): the US 10-year near 5.10 percent with a December Fed hike above 90 percent priced; Brent at USD 111; risk-off confirmed in Europe with defensives leading cyclicals on all five windows and breadth in the bottom fifth of three years; the AI complex "cracking at its financing, not its demand". The weekly digest sets Momentum at Underweight and Fragility at Double Underweight. AQ is a premium-multiple, momentum-A, AI-adjacent mid-cap, so it sits on the wrong side of both tilts; the 9 percent fall since 6 October against rising estimates is that force in action.
  • European industrial activity (biting on the base): Germany's 2026 GDP forecast cut to 0.5 percent and 2027 to 0.9 percent; high rates delaying customer investment; Eurozone manufacturing PMI 52.9 in September 2026 (highest since mid-2022) but led by AI and military investment goods. The base (trucks, agriculture, packaging, marine) is where a European slowdown would show.
  • Global capex (supporting): global capex forecasts revised to plus 20 percent in 2026 and plus 15 percent in 2027, driven by technology; North American short-cycle orders strong.
  • Sector and industry level: the Industrials digest says the market is moving from the paused-conversion cohorts toward short-cycle and newly lit acquirers; the Sector digest rates the sector's net force B (medium) and puts AQ among the designed-in names to own to 2030 through the 800V window. At the industry level, conversion and scarcity are what the market pays for.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

B78

Judgement: strongly. The sector's strongest theme ("the data centre's power and signal problem is a cable, connector, inductor and seal problem", Sector digest, 23 October 2026) names AQ explicitly and is accelerating AQ's demand faster than management expected; European defence and grid spending are second and third sector-level drivers acting on AQ at the same time.

  • Evidence of acceleration rather than mere presence: data-centre inductive share of sales from negligible in 2024 to 6 percent in Q2 2026; growth more than 60 percent in Q3; transformer output doubled sequentially in Q2; organic group growth from 9.1 to 17.9 percent between Q2 and Q3, with DNB Carnegie's data-centre forecast doubled by actuals.
  • Transmission into the P&L: volume and mix, not price. The sector digest is explicit that in data-centre components "the case is volume and mix, not price"; the CEO confirms price was on a par with or slightly below inflation in Q2. The mechanism is utilisation and design-responsible mix, which is why EBIT rose 36 percent on revenue up 21 percent.
  • Limits: the driver touches about 12 to 15 percent of sales, one customer is about 50 percent of data-centre sales, and capacity, not demand, now binds. The sector digest also warns that data-centre revenue is the least protected revenue in the sector (short contracts, build-to-specification, one or two customers, working-capital funded) and that the market can apply a lower multiple to it (Halma's Avo at 10.3 to 13.0 times EBITA against about 22 for the core).
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

C55

Judgement: four further dynamics matter, two positive and two negative, and together they roughly offset.

  • Positive: customer regionalisation of supply chains. Western OEMs are moving volume to suppliers with local footprints; AQ's local-for-local model across 17 countries and its proximity in Eastern Europe make it a natural winner (DNB Carnegie: "a significant tailwind that will help AQ Group win market share").
  • Positive: the krona translation drag has faded (SEK minus 267m in 2025, minus 113m in H1 2026, minus 3m in Q2 2026, positive in Q3 on my residual calculation); the Geography digest notes the same policy flatters results when the krona reverses. Caveat: Morgan Stanley's bullish krona view argues it may reverse again in 2027, and 73 percent of sales is non-SEK and unhedged.
  • Negative: the financing of the AI build-out is the market's main subject. Neocloud and hyperscaler credit spreads have widened (weekly digest, 23 October 2026). Demand readings are intact, but AQ's end customers' customers are the most financing-dependent buyers in the economy. The weekly digest's question "where is the growth financed, and on whose balance sheet?" lands on AQ's data-centre line: AQ funds it from operating cash and net cash, but its demand depends on hyperscaler and neocloud capex.
  • Negative: skilled-labour scarcity and turnover. Transformer winding is manual and hard to automate; 20 percent employee turnover in 2025; DNB Carnegie warns that high turnover in Pune or Cleveland could create prolonged quality and delivery bottlenecks.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

C68

Judgement: strong tailwinds, moderate headwinds, a clearly positive external picture for the next six to eighteen months, with the main external threats concentrated in two places: the multiple (via the discount rate and AI-financing sentiment) and the 2027-2028 technology re-award window.

Theme by theme:

  • AI data-centre power (sector theme 1, strengthening): strong tailwind at value-chain level (content per megawatt rising), industry level (capacity scarce, lead times long), sector level (the sector's strongest theme). Fragility: concentration in one customer and the 800V re-award.
  • European defence (strengthening): moderate tailwind; order-backed but lumpy. The sector digest's rule applies: "the backlog is safe, the quarter is not" (AQ's defence sales grew 18 percent in 2025 and disappointed management because invoicing lagged bookings; Q3 2026 deliveries slipped to Q4 on customer equipment delays).
  • Grid and electrification (stable to strengthening): moderate tailwind, long-dated, state-backed.
  • Nearshoring and regionalisation (stable): steady tailwind to share.
  • Electronics restocking pulse (6-to-18-month, now maturing): short tailwind turning neutral in 2027.
  • European heavy vehicles and industrial production (counter-theme, fading to stable): a headwind that has stopped worsening; early-upturn signs (Danske) against sequential truck-order declines (AQ).
  • Input costs (copper, steel, labour): moderate headwind, timing-driven, recovered through clauses within two quarters.
  • Macro (rates, risk-off, AI-financing scare): strong headwind to the multiple, weak headwind to earnings so far.
  • Regulation (Pillar II, EU compliance, steel duties): mild headwind, dated tax step in 2027.

Taken together, the tailwinds act on volume and mix in the highest-margin part of the business with multi-year visibility, while the headwinds act on timing (lags) and on the valuation. That asymmetry is why the external picture is more positive for earnings than for the share price.

Internal change forces

C6113 questions
C-1160C-1266C-1368C-1450C-1547C-1663C-1745C-1860C-1948C-2060C-8638C-8752C-2162

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

AQ is improving by degree, not transforming: a proven operator has raised its margin standard to above 10 percent and met it immediately, pushed its mix toward design-responsible inductive systems for data centres and defence, restarted cheap acquisitions with a capacity rationale, and institutionalised its board, all funded from operating cash. The strongest single internal force is the data-centre transformer business, because it carries both the growth and the margin claim.

The tension the Consumer must model is that the same force is where execution strain shows: delivery precision fell to 91 percent, inventory turns sit at 2.9, net working capital jumped about 3 points of sales in a year, quality issues recur in India and in new high-voltage products, and Time 24 is behind plan. AQ's history (2017, 2021-22) says such surges produce local bottlenecks that the decentralised model resolves within two to four quarters, and management's candour and fast leadership changes support that precedent. The model should therefore assume margin delivery near the new target, cash conversion below historical norms through 2026-27 while growth stays above 10 percent, acquired revenue at diluted margins converging over 12 to 24 months, and no balance-sheet or pricing lever in the base case. Growth targets (15 percent) should not be used as a forecast; the margin target is a credible floor in normal conditions.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders or other key actors changed, and what does the change signal?

C60

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement: meaningful change at board level, none at the top of management, and corrective change at site level; together they signal institutionalisation of a founder-built company and a management that acts quickly on local underperformance, not a change of direction.

  • Board (April 2026 AGM): independent director Åsa Landén Ericsson (eight years CEO of CAG Group) became chairman, replacing co-founder Claes Mellgren, who remains a non-independent director; co-founder Per Olof Andersson left the board; Roland Kasper (former Systemair Group CEO) joined. Five of six directors are independent; audit committee fully independent. This coincided with entry to Nasdaq Stockholm Large Cap (2 January 2026). It signals a deliberate move from founder governance to institutional governance while founders keep about 34 percent of the shares.
  • Management: CEO James Ahrgren (since April 2021) and CFO Christina Hegg (since 2020) unchanged; Paulina Duszel took over Electric Cabinets and System Products in June 2025; five young local leaders promoted in Q1 2026.
  • Site-level corrections in Q3 2026: India inductive factory leadership replaced after persistent quality and productivity problems; operations and finance staff sent from AQ Rockford to the underperforming Time 24 in the UK, with a new UK finance lead being hired; Dan Day (AQ Transformer Solutions, Virginia) also made managing director of Norlake.
  • What it signals: continuity of an operator CEO whose record is being extended, a board more able to challenge capital allocation, and the decentralised model's self-correction working as designed. The caution: the same names are being stretched across multiple turnarounds during the fastest ramp in the company's history (DNB Carnegie's Korsheden flags the risk of teams "spread too thin").
  • Remaining governance gaps: founder-only remuneration committee; nomination committee includes both founders (a Code deviation defended by their joint 38 percent of votes).
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced?

C66

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement: a clear, well-articulated and adequately resourced change of emphasis rather than of strategy: from build-to-print contract manufacturing toward design responsibility and complex systems, concentrated in inductive components for electrification and data centres, plus capacity for defence. The core model (decentralised, local-for-local, cost-focused, serial acquisition of cheap capacity) is unchanged.

  • Articulation: the CEO links the raised margin target directly to "taking significantly greater design responsibility than in the past" and "delivering more difficult parts with higher technology content"; the chairman frames it as growing profitably under challenging targets. Inductive components rose from 6 percent of revenue in 2018 to 25 percent in 2025; data-centre inductive from negligible to 6 percent of sales by Q2 2026.
  • Resourcing: capex SEK 241m in FY2025 (including intangibles) and SEK 201m in the first nine months of 2026; SEK 60m defence capacity in northern Sweden; SEK 23m in Hungary in H1 2026; a USD 3.7m Wytheville transformer expansion; Norlake (USD 13.5m enterprise value) doubling US inductive capacity; the 24-division reorganisation and Monitor ERP standardisation across acquired plants. All funded from operating cash; net cash still rising.
  • Gaps: the strategy does not address pricing (price is not a lever), the base's underutilised sites (Mexico, Plattsburgh) lack a plan beyond winning volume, and segment margins are not disclosed, so the market cannot verify the mix claim.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised?

C68

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement: a material, credible raise of the one target that matters most for the model (EBT margin from 8 percent to above 10 percent, 14 July 2026), already met in the quarter after it was set; the growth targets were left unchanged and remain above what the company has delivered.

  • The raise: the board lifted the EBT margin target to above 10 percent after 13 consecutive quarters above 8 percent; Q2 2026 EBT margin was 9.9 percent and Q3 10.0 percent. It is the first revision to the core margin objective in several years.
  • Reaffirmed: sales growth above 15 percent a year through the cycle (10 percent organic plus 5 percent acquired); equity ratio above 40 percent; double EPS every five years (about 14.9 percent a year), achieved between FY2021 and the twelve months to Q3 2026, with the next doubling targeted by 2031.
  • No numeric short-term guidance: the company refuses near-term forecasts; the sell-side names this as a mechanism behind regular beats.
  • Credibility: high for the margin target (it is a floor the company has already reached, and the board raised it only after three years of evidence); low for the growth target (total growth 6 percent in FY2025 and negative in FY2024 against 15 percent; the CEO's 2024 pledge to reach SEK 10bn of sales was missed at SEK 8.55bn).
  • Model implication: the raised target tells the modeller management's floor for normal conditions (EBT above 10 percent), not a forecast of upside; consensus already carries an FY2027 EBIT margin of 10.1 percent.
C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline?

C50

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement: cost discipline and governance are improving; operational execution standards are deteriorating under the strain of growth. The net is roughly neutral, and the direction of the operational KPIs is the most important near-term internal watch item.

  • Improving: EBIT margin at a record 10.1 percent with the CEO attributing it to cost control and low fixed costs rather than gross-margin gains; Monitor ERP being rolled out to acquired plants specifically to improve inventory turns and delivery; automated test and vacuum-impregnation equipment after the 2025 quality provisions; short-term incentives weighted 80 percent on EBT and 20 percent on inventory turns; board independence improved.
  • Deteriorating: delivery precision 91.1 percent in Q3 2026 (92.7 percent in Q2, 94.7 percent in Q2 2025, peak 96 percent in late 2025) against a 98 percent target; inventory turns 2.9 against 3.5; product quality 99.6 percent against 100 percent; quality provisions for historical design defects (Bulgaria, data-centre transformers) in Q4 2025; SEK 185m inventory write-downs in 2025 and SEK 10m local write-offs in India and Bulgaria; 75 lost-time accidents in 2025.
  • Pattern: the Risks report identifies a recurring pattern of local bottlenecks during growth surges (2017 express-transport costs; 90 percent delivery precision in 2022; 92 percent in 2021) systematically resolved within a few quarters through local reinvestment. Precedent supports recovery; the scale of the current ramp (and its concentration in novel, high-voltage products where a quality failure has reputational consequences) makes this episode riskier than the last.
C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units?

❌ D47

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement: hardly at all. The perimeter is expanding (Time 24, Gumboda, Norlake in 2026; semiconductors as a new vertical), and the only disposal is non-operating (Gävle property, SEK 26m cash, SEK 22m gain). The 24-division reorganisation and ERP standardisation simplify management, not the portfolio.

  • The low-return units (Mexico and Plattsburgh wiring, India inductive, Time 24) are being fixed, not exited; AQ's culture and record (mdexx from minus 7 to plus 7 percent EBT within a year) favour turnaround over exit, which has worked historically but takes management bandwidth.
  • Contrast with peers: Bekaert closed 13 plants since 2019 and exited Latin American wire; SFS is consolidating five sites (about 650 jobs) and sold Allchemet; both used perimeter reduction as a margin lever. AQ has not needed to, because its margin progress came from mix and cost, but it also means a wide, complex perimeter persists (17 countries, more than 40 companies).
  • For the model: no disposal effects; continued small acquisitions add to the perimeter.
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly?

C63

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Judgement: active and mostly sensible entry into close adjacencies that share AQ's customers, skills and footprint; the one less-close move (UK semiconductors via Time 24) is the one that is struggling.

  • Sensible, close to the core: US inductive capacity (Norlake, Cleveland, up to USD 35m sales at full utilisation, under the Virginia team, filled with AQ's own backlogged volumes); medium-voltage data-centre transformers (inductive know-how, ABB as customer and route to market); solid-state transformer prototypes; defence heavy mechanics and wiring (existing sheet-metal and wiring skills, Swedish customers); utility-scale battery-storage enclosures (sheet metal; up to EUR 40m annual sales per factory at full utilisation, per a 2022 call); e-truck harnesses.
  • Less close: Time 24 (UK cleanroom systems and electrical cabinets for semiconductor and railway customers; about SEK 124m sales at below-group margins). Output fell short of expectations in Q3 2026 and semiconductor customers are delaying investment.
  • The Wisdom Library frame ("core versus adjacency growth") supports the judgement: moves that share customers, channels and skills succeed far more often; AQ's data-centre and defence moves share all three, Time 24 shares skills only.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

❌ D45

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Judgement: AQ is not raising prices in real terms; pricing is nominal pass-through of metal costs with a lag, and real pricing power is at most latent in the data-centre niche. Management confirms this directly, so the modeller should carry price as zero-to-slightly-negative in real terms for the base and as a possible, unproven upside in data centres.

  • Direct evidence: the CEO said on the Q2 2026 call that price adjustments were a very small part of the 9.9 percent sales growth and on a par with or slightly below general inflation; growth was volume-driven, unlike 2022 when organic growth was largely price.
  • Structural cap: OEMs demand 3 to 5 percent annual productivity cuts; termination-for-convenience clauses and dual-sourcing; no intellectual property (the BF report's "central structural vulnerability").
  • Latent upside: design responsibility in custom inductive systems "secures stronger pricing power than traditional assembly" (CF report); "winning every US quote" in a capacity-constrained market suggests AQ could price higher; management has not said so and the margin explanation it gives is cost and mix, not price. The sector digest says designed-in custom parts can raise prices in a restocking pulse while standard parts pass costs late; AQ sits on both sides.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they?

C60

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement: M&A has restarted after a full year of deliberate inactivity, the deals are small, cheap and strategically sensible, and integration quality is mixed: excellent at mdexx, poor so far at Time 24. The cadence (one meaningful deal and two bolt-ons in 2026) is well below the stated two to four factories a year and 5 percent acquired growth.

  • Discipline: no deals in 2025 because seller multiples exceeded internal thresholds; net cash built to about SEK 700m by Q1 2026. Historic price about 6 times EBITA (eight deals since 2019); the Industry digest notes that small acquirers paying 3 to 7 times in fragmented niches enjoy the widest arbitrage. Management says defence multiples are now excessive and it will not overpay.
  • 2026 deals: Time 24 (May; £1 plus about SEK 50m debt redemption; SEK 124m sales; dilutive; underperforming in Q3); Gumboda Stålteknik (Q3; SEK 2m; SEK 14m sales; folded into JIT Mech for defence and electrification capacity); Norlake (21 September; USD 1.4m cash plus about USD 12m liabilities assumed, USD 13.5m enterprise value including facilities valued at about USD 7.3m; capacity for up to SEK 300m sales). Acquired growth contributed 0.9 percent in Q2 and 2.2 percent in Q3.
  • Integration record: mdexx (bought January 2025 for SEK 144m; diluted group margin by about 0.5 to 1.0 points in 2025; personnel costs cut 10 percent; EBT from minus 7 to plus 7 percent; management sees up to 15 percent) and Riedel (stabilised after a 2025 hailstorm; above target margin) are successes. The Risks report's base rate is two to four years for acquired units to reach group margins historically, accelerating to 12 to 18 months recently (DNB Carnegie).
  • Model implication: acquired revenue arrives at below-group margins and converges over 12 to 24 months; the DNB Carnegie long-term scenario assumes 8 percent acquired growth a year, which the 2025-26 record does not support; 2 to 4 percent a year is the evidenced range.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet, or stepping up shareholder returns, or is management increasingly mooting either?

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Judgement: very little. The balance sheet is strong and under-used by design; shareholder returns are a small progressive dividend; management has never bought back shares and is not mooting either a buyback or leverage.

  • Balance sheet: net cash SEK 817m excluding leases at Q2 2026 (SEK 591m a year earlier); company-reported net cash SEK 467m at Q3 2026 including leases; equity ratio 67 to 68 percent against a 40 percent floor; net debt to EBITDA minus 0.38x (FactSet, including leases). The capital-structure target was kept unchanged in July 2026.
  • Returns: dividend SEK 1.80 for 2025 (up 12.5 percent; SEK 165m paid in April 2026), policy about 25 percent of cycle-average profit, historically paid at about 19 percent; consensus DPS SEK 2.12 for 2026. Never a buyback, "citing structurally low share liquidity"; warrant programmes add up to under 1 percent dilution each.
  • What is rising is capital spending, not distribution: capex and small acquisitions absorb most free cash. At 2 times EBITDA the company could deploy roughly SEK 3.5bn (calculated: about 2 x SEK 1.37bn FY2026 EBITDA plus existing net cash), which at 6 times EBITA would add earnings of the order of 50 percent; nothing in management's behaviour suggests it will. This is a latent EPS lever for scenario analysis, not a base case.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

C60

Judgement: several smaller positives reinforce the main ones.

  • Large Cap entry (2 January 2026): broader institutional eligibility and liquidity (average daily value traded USD 4 to 6m).
  • Capacity investment ahead of 2027: management expects further transformer capacity expansions in 2027 as more hyperscale customers adopt dry-type transformers; capacity is the binding constraint, so each step converts directly into revenue.
  • Technology step-up: solid-state transformer prototypes for a leading electrification customer with production expected 2027-2028; supercapacitor cabinets for grid balancing (orders of EUR 4m); 3D co-design with customers.
  • ERP standardisation (Monitor) across acquired plants: a mechanical improvement in planning, inventory and delivery that compounds with each acquisition.
  • Energy cost actions: solar installations at Indian and Bulgarian sites with short paybacks; 100 percent renewable electricity in Swedish plants (a customer requirement).
  • Plausible: a capital markets day or a further target revision once the 10 percent EBT margin is sustained for a full year (the company has never printed a full-year EBT margin above 9.6 percent); a step-up in acquisitions using the net cash.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

❌ D38

Judgement: a cluster of execution strains, each manageable alone, that together form the most credible near-term internal risk: growth is outrunning the organisation's capacity to deliver it at the required quality.

  • Present: delivery precision at 91.1 percent (Q3 2026), the lowest since 2022, which management calls unacceptable; the India inductive factory's persistent quality and productivity deficits (leadership replaced); Time 24 underperforming; Mexico and Plattsburgh wiring underutilised with recovery "slower than desired"; net working capital up to 31.9 percent of trailing revenue from 28.7 percent a year earlier, with inventory turns at 2.9; data-centre quality defects and provisions in late 2025; 20 percent staff turnover and a lost-time injury frequency rate of 6.
  • Plausible: a warranty or redesign event on a medium-voltage transformer (the Risks report rates it the highest-plausibility failure mode because it already happened once); managers stretched across turnarounds (Dan Day running Virginia and Norlake during a ramp); a founder share sale after a large share-price rise (named as a perception trigger in the memo); acquisitions that dilute margins for longer than the recent 12-to-18-month norm.
  • Governance, unchanged: warrants with no performance conditions, clawbacks or holding periods, retained after leaving; a founder-only remuneration committee.
  • Wisdom Library fit: "Pret sandwich risk" and "cockroach at M&A integration level" are forming at an early, not alarming, stage.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

C52

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

Judgement: none indicated. The founders (Mellgren and Andersson, about 34 percent of shares, 34 to 38 percent of votes) have stepped back from board leadership but show no sign of wanting a different strategy, a sale or a capital-return policy; there is no activist on the register; the free float is about 60 percent.

  • The founders' joint stake blocks any hostile approach and lets them defend the nomination-committee deviation. The new independent chairman has publicly endorsed the existing direction (profitable growth under challenging targets).
  • No insider selling in the last twelve months; CEO holds 114,840 shares, CFO 25,000.
  • The plausible future change is generational (eventual founder selling), which would be a sentiment event rather than a strategic one.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C62

Judgement: moderately strong, fairly narrow and well resourced. Three internal forces are strong (the margin-target raise backed by delivery; the data-centre transformer entry with design responsibility; resumed, disciplined M&A with a capacity rationale), supported by board institutionalisation and capacity investment. They are narrow in that most of the change concentrates in one product area (inductive components for electrification) and one country step (the US). They are well resourced because every one of them is funded from operating cash with net cash still rising.

What holds the rating below "strong and broad": the change is an improvement of degree on an existing compounding engine, not a transformation; there is no pricing change, no perimeter simplification, no balance-sheet change; and the operational KPIs that should accompany a step-up in standards are moving the wrong way. The positives are larger than the negatives (the margin and growth evidence is in the P&L the negatives are in KPIs and one quarter's cash), but the negatives are the ones that would surface first if the ramp stumbles.
