Industry and Value Chain

C5416 questions
C-2452C-2545C-2660C-2766C-2842C-2943C-3145C-8452C-3266C-3358C-3457C-3552C-3650C-3757C-3858C-3947

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

AQ operates in an industry whose structure does not give suppliers pricing power: a fragmented, slowly consolidating base of contract manufacturers sells to concentrated OEMs who dual-source, demand 3 to 5 percent annual productivity cuts and contract for convenience, and competitors turn volume-first when demand falls. AQ's above-industry margins (9 to 10 percent against a 7 to 8 percent norm) and its share gains come from being among the most advantaged consolidators (net cash, low-cost footprint, cost culture, a cheap acquire-and-fill playbook), not from industry structure.

The supportive exception is the data-centre and electrification niche, where demand exceeds qualified capacity, equipment lead times are long and AQ is winning every US quote; but this support is a capacity cycle with a closing date (new capacity in 2027-2029) and a technology re-award window (800V DC and solid-state transformers, 2026-2028), and it runs through a single gatekeeper customer (ABB, about 50 percent of AQ's data-centre sales). The precedents in this sector say that AQ has already received the relabelling re-rating and that data-centre revenue of this form (short contracts, one customer, working-capital funded) is the kind the market eventually values lower. For the model: no price-led margin expansion; margin near 10 percent sustained by mix and utilisation; growth in the niche bounded by capacity steps; and a downside case for the base in which competitors' volume-first behaviour returns.

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

C52

Judgement: AQ is a top-tier player among Nordic contract manufacturers and a niche leader in a few places (US medium-voltage data-centre magnetics, where it is currently winning every quote it submits), but in European contract manufacturing as a whole it is a mid-sized participant with a low share, far from any dominant position. The files contain no hard market-share data; this judgement rests on scale, peer comparisons and qualitative evidence, and the modeller should not assume share-driven pricing power.

  • Scale: SEK 9.1bn revenue (2025), 7,848 staff, 17 countries, market capitalisation about SEK 20.8bn (Large Cap since January 2026). DNB Carnegie and the BF report describe AQ as leading the Nordic tier-two consolidator cohort (HANZA, Inission, Scanfil, Note, Kitron, Incap) on acquisitions (eight since 2019) and technology breadth.
  • Niche positions: inductive components are about 25 percent of revenue (2025) and grew at a 30 percent compound rate 2018-2024; in data-centre medium-voltage UPS transformers AQ is a key supplier into ABB's HiPerGuard and serves five to six data-centre customers, with capacity of 70 systems a month (Q2 2026) and the US capacity being doubled.
  • Relative to customers: AQ is small against its OEM customers (Volvo, Scania, ABB, Siemens, Alstom, Hitachi), which is the relationship that sets price.
  • Distance from number one or two: in the European EMS market (about 1,900 companies; the largest 112 hold 89 percent of sales) AQ is one of many mid-sized players; there is no evidence it is number one or two in any broad product market, only in specific customer programmes.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution?

❌ D45

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Judgement: on the supply side the market is fragmented at the bottom and moderately concentrated at the top, and concentration is rising; on the demand side it is highly concentrated, and that asymmetry is what caps AQ's pricing. Top-five shares are not in the files.

  • Supply side: over 1,900 European EMS companies, 94 percent small non-integrated factories; the largest 112 raised their combined share from 79 to 89 percent over a decade. That is a steep distribution (a long tail of very small firms), but the files do not show whether the top five exceed 30 percent; given the number of mid-sized players (Zollner, Kitron, Scanfil, Note, HANZA and others), I would not assume it.
  • Data-centre magnetics: capacity-constrained with a few qualified suppliers per OEM programme and six-year equipment lead times (Morgan Stanley), which behaves like a concentrated market in the short run.
  • Demand side: John Deere holds 57 percent of US agricultural equipment and Caterpillar 23 percent of North American construction (Comer's BF report, same customers); in Europe a handful of truck OEMs; in data centres one customer is about 50 percent of AQ's segment sales. AQ's top ten customers are just over half of its sales; the largest SEK 1,012m (11 percent); the top two 18 percent (2025, down from 20 percent).
  • Implication: a fragmented, consolidating supplier industry selling to concentrated buyers does not support supplier pricing; it supports consolidators that win share through cost, reliability and capacity, which is AQ's model.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

C60

Judgement: shares are shifting slowly and persistently toward larger, better-capitalised consolidators; new entrants have taken negligible share; AQ has been a share gainer.

  • The 79-to-89 percent consolidation of the top 112 European EMS companies over a decade is the measure of the shift. Customer relationships are sticky (decades-long; five-to-six-year vehicle programmes; co-engineering), so share moves through programme awards and acquisitions, not through rapid switching.
  • New entrants: none material in European contract manufacturing; Asian low-cost suppliers compete at the edge (standard parts); Chinese OEM entry into European industrial niches is a risk the Risks report rates medium. In data-centre power, automotive suppliers (Aptiv, Lear) could extend 800V EV know-how into data-centre power conversion, but Morgan Stanley judges qualification hurdles high and intrusion slow.
  • AQ's share gains: management claims it is taking share from direct competitors (hard to prove at customer level, but consistent with 17.9 percent organic growth against flat organic growth at Addtech and 1 percent at Note in Q2 2026, and Incap's fourth consecutive year of organic decline).
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones, and is this company among them?

C66

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Judgement: consolidation runs toward the most advantaged rather than simply the largest, and AQ is among the most advantaged in its cohort on four counts: net cash, a low-cost Eastern European footprint, a proven cheap-acquisition-and-fill playbook, and a cost culture that sustains margins of 9 to 10 percent against a 7 to 8 percent industry norm.

  • Mechanism: in downturns leveraged competitors cannot finance capacity; AQ buys distressed capacity at about 6 times EBITA and fills it with group volume (DNB Carnegie: the M&A strategy "allows the company to capture market share faster than peers by adding capacity and technical expertise during cyclical industrial slowdowns when competitors face capital constraints"). Customers' regionalisation pushes volume toward suppliers with local footprints.
  • Evidence AQ is among the advantaged: 117 consecutive profitable quarters; EBIT margin 9.3 percent (FY2025) against Nordic peers' typical 7 to 8 percent and HANZA's sub-4 percent margins in 2016-2022; net debt to EBITDA minus 0.4x against peer leverage of 0.6 to 2.2x in the peer table and Incap and Note under margin pressure.
  • Limit: advantage in this industry is operational and must be re-earned each cycle; it does not protect margin from OEMs, it protects share and survival. This is why the Wisdom Library's "fragmentation is usually structural, not a roll-up opportunity" is only partly rebutted here: consolidation is happening, but it confers scale and cost, not pricing.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: margin-first or volume-first; benign or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

❌ D42

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: competitors are volume-first in downturns and margin-first only where capacity is short; the industry is not benign; value-added prices do fall (or productivity cuts bite harder) when volume falls, while metal prices pass through mechanically in both directions.

  • Evidence: the memo records that competitors prioritise volume in downturns; Indian price competition cut Incap's EBITA margin to 9.0 percent from a 13.4 percent historical median, with existing customers also squeezing renewals (Inderes); regional pump makers grew units 30 percent by undercutting (Comer's file, adjacent industry); Asian producers' price-volume strategies in tyre cord (Bekaert's file). OEMs demand 3 to 5 percent annual productivity cuts regardless.
  • Where it is benign: consolidated niches (fifth wheels and landing gears in Comer's file) and capacity-constrained data-centre magnetics, where incumbents are winning every quote and the issue is delivery, not price.
  • Prices and volume: AQ's history shows revenue falling with metal deflation (2024) while EBIT rose, which means the value-added price did not collapse in the 2024 downturn; the risk is that a deeper downturn with spare capacity across Eastern Europe would bring the "shark attack" the next question asks about.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability, and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

❌ D43

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: moderately unstable and situation-dependent. Over the last eighteen months to three years the attitude has hardened at the low-cost edge (India) and among capacity-adding Nordic peers, while softening in capacity-constrained niches. A shark attack on AQ's core pricing is plausible in a demand downturn, not in today's environment.

  • Instability signals: Incap's margin collapse (2025-26) from competitive intensity in India shows that a low-cost hub can turn quickly; Scanfil's new 15 percent growth target with 7 to 8 percent EBITA margins (late-2026 capital markets day) signals volume ambition aimed at AQ's electrification and medtech markets; Tamura's Mexico expansion targets the same US transformer demand.
  • Stabilising factors: high switching costs on co-engineered parts; customer preference for proven quality (AQ's 2023 loss of a SEK 250m battery account over quality shows the punishment is for quality failure, not price); equipment lead times.
  • Shark-attack exposure: highest in standard harnesses and sheet metal where Mexico and Plattsburgh are already underutilised; lowest in custom inductive systems. AQ's weakness that a competitor could exploit is delivery precision at 91 percent, not price.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

❌ D45

Judgement: the industry structure is mildly unsupportive for pricing and margin, supportive for a disciplined consolidator's share and growth, and the support that exists in AQ's growth niche is temporary. AQ earns above-industry margins because of what it does, not because of where it sits.

  • Unsupportive features: price-taking sub-supplier to concentrated OEMs who dual-source and contract for convenience; no intellectual property; the OEMs' own aftermarket margins (about 26 percent) give them every reason to squeeze; volume-first competitors in downturns; a fragmented supplier base.
  • Supportive features: consolidation toward advantaged players; customer regionalisation; high co-engineering switching costs; capacity scarcity and long equipment lead times in data-centre power.
  • Fragility: the niche support is a capacity cycle that closes as new capacity arrives in 2027-2029 (Industry digest: "the slot closes when new capacity lands in 2027 to 2029") and as 800V DC re-awards designs; the base's support depends on European OEM volumes.
  • Peer comparison: Daetwyler's file shows the same industry logic in pharma elastomers (a dominant leader, West, takes 80 to 90 percent of new molecules; a second-source supplier must discount 30 to 50 percent); AQ is not in that position in data centres yet, but one customer controlling 50 percent of its segment sales is the precondition for it.
C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

C52

Judgement: in this industry outperformance comes from three sources (a demand pulse in a niche, a relabelling of the multiple, and cheap serial acquisition with operational fill), and underperformance from the reverse (pulse reverting, multiple reverting to the contract-manufacturer label, an acquisition or quality stumble). AQ fits all three outperformance patterns and is now at the stage where the second (relabelling) has largely been paid for and the first (the pulse) is near its peak rate of change; the third (compounding through acquisitions) is the durable one.

  • Pattern 1, demand pulse: Kitron's defence-led 71.7 percent revenue growth (Q2 2026); the sector digest's restocking pulse of six to eighteen months; AQ's own 2022-23 boom (organic 20 percent-plus, price-led) followed by minus 6.9 percent in 2024 and a share price near SEK 132 in early 2025. Rule (Wisdom Library "demand pulse reversion", "lazy cyclical extrapolation"): pulses revert and markets over-extrapolate them.
  • Pattern 2, relabelling: the Sector digest says "relabelling moves the multiple far more than earnings" and that designed-in European mid-caps are "still valued as cable assemblers rather than on the Amphenol label" (Volex at 10.2 times operating profit). AQ is the opposite case: it has already been relabelled; its 24-month-forward P/E ranged from 15.5 to 25.7 times over five years and touched the 91st to 97th percentile in early October 2026. The Industry digest's counter-rule is that the fastest-growing data-centre revenue is the least protected (short contracts, one or two customers, working-capital funded) and "deserves a lower multiple": Halma's photonics unit at 10 to 13 times EBITA against 22 for the core. AQ fits that warning (one customer about 50 percent of data-centre sales; net working capital up 3 points of sales).
  • Pattern 3, serial acquisition: small acquirers paying 3 to 7 times EBIT in fragmented niches enjoy the widest arbitrage (Judges Scientific, Volex at 5.2 times EBITDA; AQ at about 6 times EBITA); the warning sign is a rising entry multiple with drifting returns (Halma's ROIC from 16.3 to 14.4 percent). AQ's after-tax ROIC rose from 11.9 percent (2019) to 16.7 percent (2025) while buying at stable multiples, the healthy version.
  • Underperformance precedents in the peer files: Daetwyler's post-Covid destocking (EPS from CHF 3.38 to 1.89 in a year; a 20.8 times EV/EBIT multiple compressing); SFS and Bekaert de-rated on European industrial weakness and relied on footprint cuts; Comer's 23 percent revenue fall in 2024 on agricultural destocking. AQ's own EPS rose through 2024 (SEK 6.91 to 7.27), which is the strongest empirical evidence that it is a compounder rather than a pure cyclical.
  • Operational rule of thumb: EMS margins of 7 to 8 percent are the norm and about 10 percent EBIT has been called a peak for contract manufacturers (DNB Carnegie, before the target raise); the Risks report puts further structural upside under 100 basis points. AQ is at that ceiling; further margin gains need mix (design responsibility) to change the ceiling, not utilisation alone.

For the Consumer, the precedents imply: model the growth slice with a pulse shape (fast, then capacity-bound, then exposed to re-award), the base with a cyclical shape, the acquisition engine as a steady 2 to 4 percent of revenue a year at diluted then converging margins, and assume margins near but not much above 10 percent.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

C66

Judgement: customer demand strongly supports the growth thesis today and is fragile in two specific ways: concentration in one data-centre customer and dependence of that customer's demand on hyperscaler and neocloud capital spending, whose financing is now the market's main worry.

  • Supportive: data-centre customer forecasts rising faster than management expected; five to six data-centre customers; defence customers accelerating schedules; construction equipment orders resilient in Europe; HVAC and gas-turbine components growing on data-centre demand; new e-truck harness nomination; grid projects recovering. Organic growth 17.9 percent in Q3 2026.
  • Weak spots: heavy trucks (sequential order declines), buses in North America, agriculture, marine and food packaging; semiconductor customers delaying investment (Time 24).
  • Fragility 1, concentration: one legacy electrification customer (from the evidence, ABB) is about 50 percent of AQ's data-centre sales; AQ mitigates by delivering into several business areas within that customer. A redesign, insourcing or dual-sourcing decision there would hit the highest-margin growth line.
  • Fragility 2, financing: the weekly digest records hyperscaler and neocloud credit spreads widening and Goldman Sachs' risk appetite indicator negative; no house has found slowing compute demand yet, but a capex pause would transmit to AQ's data-centre inductive line within about two quarters (Risks report).
  • Fragility 3, contract form: major industrial customers insist on termination-for-convenience clauses; order books are short.
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

C58

Judgement: the route to market helps AQ and is simple (direct to OEMs, no distributors), with one important exception: in data centres AQ reaches hyperscalers through ABB, which is simultaneously its largest data-centre customer, its route to market and a potential competitor for the value.

  • Direct model: AQ sells directly to blue-chip OEMs with no intermediaries, capturing the full contract value and staying close to design decisions (the BF report counts this as margin capture).
  • ABB as gatekeeper: sell-side analysts call AQ a "hidden" beneficiary of power-infrastructure capex via ABB; ABB's HiPerGuard medium-voltage UPS (launched April 2026) and its NVIDIA collaboration (June 2026) are AQ's channel to hyperscale deployments. This is a strength while ABB wins and a single point of dependence if ABB redesigns, dual-sources or insources the magnetics.
  • Fragility: low for the base (many OEMs, direct), medium-to-high for the data-centre line (one gatekeeper). The Deutsche Bank warning that value migrates toward large electrical vendors with grid-to-rack breadth describes exactly ABB's position relative to AQ.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

C57

Judgement: competitor behaviour currently helps AQ on balance (several peers are struggling or slow), but the strongest competitors are expanding into AQ's growth markets, so the advantage is time-limited; AQ's position is moderately robust in the base and fragile only if its delivery problems persist.

  • Helping: Incap's profit warning and fourth year of organic decline; Note's 1 percent organic growth (Q2 2026) and margin dilution from acquisitions; Componenta's sub-5 percent historical EBIT margins; Schaffner's inability to match AQ's Bulgarian cost base (DNB Carnegie).
  • Hurting or threatening: Kitron's defence momentum (it took the top spot in the CF report's peer momentum ranking; AQ second); Scanfil's growth and capacity push into electrification; KB Components' roll-up strategy in components; Tamura's Mexico expansion; Addtech also benefiting from data-centre construction.
  • AQ's relative position: EBIT margin 9.9 percent against Note's 7.7 percent in Q2 2026; best-in-class returns against Scanfil and Inission (BF report). DNB Carnegie warns that if integration delays at Cleveland slow deliveries to backlogged hyperscale customers, more agile local competitors could take share; delivery precision of 91 percent is the vulnerability.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

C52

Judgement: the new-entrant threat is low in the base and medium in the data-centre niche over two to four years, with the largest threat coming not from new contract manufacturers but from changes in who designs the power architecture.

  • Adjacent entrants (medium, slow): automotive suppliers such as Aptiv and Lear extending 800V EV engineering into data-centre power conversion (Morgan Stanley; qualification hurdles high, intrusion takes significant time); large transformer makers adding US capacity (Tamura).
  • Standard entrants (low): a new European contract manufacturer needs years of qualification, co-engineering and capital; entry barriers are described as high; the European industry is consolidating, not attracting entry.
  • Low-cost foreign entrants (medium at the edge): Chinese OEMs or suppliers offering industrial components up to 30 percent below regional peers (Risks report, medium plausibility); mitigated by customers' regionalisation and tariff walls.
  • Orthogonal or innovative (medium-to-high, 2027-2030): a shift to DC-native, centralised power architectures and solid-state transformers could move value to large electrical vendors with grid-to-rack breadth and bypass discrete magnetics suppliers unless designed in early (Deutsche Bank). This is less an entrant than a redefinition of the product.
  • Customer insourcing (low): viable only at mass-production volumes, which AQ's high-mix, low-volume programmes largely avoid (BF report); Caterpillar and John Deere building electric powertrain capability is a Comer-specific example.
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives a threat, and how is that changing?

C50

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement: substitution is a low threat to the base and a rising, medium-term, two-sided threat to the data-centre line through solid-state transformers and 800V DC.

  • Data centres: solid-state transformers replace conventional magnetic transformers with power electronics plus smaller high-frequency magnetics; 800V DC removes conversion stages. Both reduce or change the conventional magnetic content per megawatt, but the sector digest's content estimates rise (about USD 3.8m per megawatt with solid-state transformers against about USD 2.5m today), so the total opportunity grows while the specific product changes. AQ's SST prototypes position it on the substitute side; whether it wins series production (expected 2027-2028) is unknown.
  • Cooling: solid-state chip cooling is under 5 percent of the liquid-cooling market (TTB Wealth); a rapid shift could reduce HVAC component demand, a small exposure.
  • Base: substitution of wiring harnesses or sheet metal is not a material threat; vehicle electrification increases harness and enclosure content (battery enclosures).
  • Change over time: the threat rises from negligible today to material in the 2028-2030 window; it is not in the 18-month model, but it governs the terminal growth of the data-centre line and therefore the multiple.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

C57

Judgement: on balance regulators and politicians help AQ's demand and hurt its costs and tax; the demand support (defence, grid, nearshoring) is durable for several years, while the cost and tax burdens are dated and modest.

  • Helping: defence budgets and security-of-supply procurement (Componenta's EUR 20.5m Finnish defence contract is a parallel); grid investment by Svenska kraftnät and Fingrid; domestic-sourcing rules favouring tariff-insulated local suppliers; US tariffs that favour local production (AQ's US transformer capacity sits inside the wall).
  • Hurting: Pillar II additional tax from 2027; EU Steel Overcapacity Regulation duties and documentation (from July and October 2026); CSRD reporting and potential CBAM costs; HSE legislation that could require capital spending; customer-mandated carbon reduction (AQ targets 4 percent annual reduction in emissions relative to sales through 2034; customers control 80 percent of value-chain emissions through material choice); the Texas data-centre permitting pause (September 2026).
  • Fragility: low for defence and grid (multi-year, state-backed, and accelerating), medium for US data-centre permitting (local politics around power availability), low for tax (known and dated).
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting, how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

C58

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement: the disruption forces act most powerfully on demand (positively) and on the architecture of AQ's customers' products (two-sided), less on suppliers (cost inflation) and regulators (supportive); they leave AQ in a strong position for 18 months and a contested one in 2028-2030.

  • Demand (end customers: hyperscalers, defence ministries, grid operators): AI compute, rearmament and grid expansion raise volumes; positive, strong.
  • AQ's direct customers (ABB and other electrification OEMs): being reshaped by DC architectures and digital-twin design with NVIDIA; they will decide which magnetics suppliers are designed into the next generation; two-sided.
  • Competition: capacity additions by peers and adjacent entrants close the current scarcity window from 2027; negative over time.
  • Suppliers: copper demand from grid and data centres keeps copper near records (Goldman Sachs USD 13,800 for 2027); AI-driven electronic component inflation; equipment lead times above six years (Morgan Stanley) limit everyone's capacity additions, which protects incumbents; mixed.
  • Regulators: supportive on demand, burdensome on cost.
  • Fragility: the position is fragile only at the customer-architecture node; if AQ is designed into SST and 800V platforms, disruption extends its growth; if not, the data-centre line plateaus after 2028.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

❌ D47

Judgement: suppliers are a moderate source of cost risk and a negligible source of availability or concentration risk.

  • AQ buys semi-finished steel, aluminium, copper winding wire, cable and electronic components locally from many suppliers, with a dual-sourcing policy; no single-supplier dependency is disclosed; central purchasing gives scale benefits against smaller rivals.
  • The risk is price: copper and steel at multi-year highs, steel duties from July 2026, electronic component inflation, and supplier delivery times lengthening (Zeus, October 2026). Pass-through clauses recover these with a 3-to-6-month lag, and AQ holds no hedges.
  • Capital-equipment suppliers matter for capacity: winding and vacuum-impregnation machinery, large CNC and welding cells, a Mazak 15kW fibre laser; long lead times constrain how fast AQ (and competitors) can add data-centre capacity.

Company - Competitive advantage

C567 questions
C-4158C-4257C-4370C-4458C-4538C-4658C-4755

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

AQ's competitive advantage is real, moderately strong and operational: a cost culture with decentralised accountability, a low-cost local-for-local footprint, co-engineered customer relationships and a repeatable cheap-acquisition-and-fill playbook. These advantages win share and protect profitability through downturns (117 consecutive profitable quarters, EPS up through 2024's minus 6.9 percent organic year), and the shift toward design responsibility in inductive systems is slowly adding a customer-related advantage on top. They do not create pricing power: AQ has no intellectual property, sells to concentrated OEMs who dual-source and demand productivity cuts, and prices at or below inflation, so it shares most cost gains with customers.

For the Consumer this means: the margin is capped near 10 percent with upside only from mix, not price; revenue growth can run well above market because the addressable market is vast relative to AQ and consolidation favours it; the durability of the advantage is tied to the operator and the culture (an execution moat), which is why management quality carries more weight in this case than in a structurally moated business; and the near-term weak point in customer utility is delivery precision, which must recover for the "not inferior" verdict to hold through the 2027-2028 re-award window.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold, how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

C58

Question asked Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement: AQ's advantages are operational and cultural, not structural; together they are moderately strong, and on balance they are widening slightly because design responsibility in inductive systems adds a customer-related advantage the company did not have five years ago. There is no intellectual-property, regulatory or network moat, which is why bull and bear research alike cap the sustainable margin near 10 percent.

By type:

  • Customer-related (moderate, widening): co-engineered products embedded in customers' systems from the design phase; five-to-six-year vehicle programmes; decades-long relationships with ABB, Alstom, Hitachi, Scania, Volvo; customer qualification cycles. Widening through greater design responsibility in inductive components (25 percent of revenue in 2025 from 6 percent in 2018) and early co-design with 3D tools. Limited by termination-for-convenience clauses and dual-sourcing.
  • Production-related (moderate-to-strong, stable): a low-cost Eastern European footprint (about 65 percent of staff in Bulgaria, Poland, Lithuania, Estonia), local-for-local plants in 17 countries that customers value for responsiveness, tariff insulation and lower transport emissions; flexible relocation of work between units (about 10 percent of production dual-sourced). Narrowed slightly by Eastern European wage inflation.
  • Scale-based (moderate, widening): central procurement, the capacity to buy and fill distressed plants, the balance sheet to add capacity in downturns when rivals cannot.
  • Regulatory or political (weak): none beyond the nearshoring and domestic-sourcing tailwind.
  • Culture and knowledge (strong, stable): decentralised profit-and-loss accountability across more than 40 companies, "simple and cost-effective, which means we are competitive", 117 consecutive profitable quarters through two severe downturns, a repeatable turnaround playbook (mdexx from minus 7 to plus 7 percent EBT within a year). This is the hardest to copy and the most important.
  • Wisdom Library check ("moats and SRCA", "six-level moat hierarchy"): this is an execution moat (re-earned each cycle) rather than a legacy moat; its durability depends on the operator, which the management group addresses.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors, and what does that scale confer?

C57

Question asked How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

Judgement: AQ is large enough among Nordic contract manufacturers to confer procurement, footprint and acquisition advantages, and small enough against its customers that scale confers no pricing power.

  • Size: SEK 9.1bn revenue (2025; about SEK 10.2bn consensus for 2026), 7,848 employees (June 2026), more than 40 operating companies, 24 divisions, seven product areas, 17 countries; market capitalisation about SEK 20.8bn. Against Nordic peers it is among the largest and the most diversified technologically; Addtech's earnings are nearly three times AQ's (a different, distribution-led model).
  • What it confers: purchasing scale on semi-finished metals; the ability to move work between plants to offset local inflation, tariffs and currency; capacity to finance two to four acquisitions a year from cash; the credibility to be qualified by blue-chip OEMs for system and data-centre programmes; a Large Cap listing.
  • What it does not confer: price leadership; the customers are far larger (Volvo, Scania, ABB, Siemens) and set terms. Scale advantage over named rivals is real but not large enough to make AQ a cost leader by a wide margin outside its Eastern European plants (the Wisdom Library's "scale needs a bounded market" test is not met: the market is unbounded and rivals can grow).
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

C70

Judgement: the addressable market is many times AQ's size and does not constrain growth; the constraint is AQ's capacity, execution and acquisition cadence. The mid-term market growth accessible to AQ supports 8 to 12 percent total growth a year (organic plus acquired), with the structural slice able to grow 20 percent-plus for several years.

  • Base market: European contract manufacturing of components and systems (over 1,900 companies; AQ well below 5 percent of it on any reasonable estimate) plus the Americas and Asia; growth roughly in line with industrial production (low single digits) plus outsourcing and regionalisation share shifts.
  • Structural niches: data-centre power content rising per megawatt to about 2030 (Sector digest); 800V DC possibly 40 to 50 percent of AI data-centre power architectures by 2030 (ABB); European defence multi-year; Nordic grid EUR 25bn over 2026-35; utility-scale battery storage. AQ's data-centre business, about 6 percent of group sales, can double more than once before the market is a constraint.
  • Acquisition pool: a fragmented market of small, often family-owned factories in Europe, India, China and the Americas; AQ buys about 6 times EBITA; the constraint is seller willingness and price, not supply of targets.
  • Implication: the company's 15 percent growth target is not TAM-constrained; its repeated misses come from the cycle and from capacity and acquisition discipline.
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior?

C58

Question asked How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

Judgement: demonstrably not inferior, and leading on responsiveness, local presence, cost and engineering partnership; currently below standard on delivery reliability and, in pockets, on quality. The aim at this stage is to disprove inferiority, and the evidence does so.

  • Evidence of superior utility: customers keep awarding AQ programmes through cycles; AQ is winning every US data-centre transformer quote (DNB Carnegie); new e-truck harness and sheet-metal nomination; a SEK 100m three-year agreement with a world-leading electrification customer (Q1 2025) and a EUR 15m medium-voltage transformer order for a hyperscale data centre; the local-for-local model reduces transport, lead time and customer carbon footprint; the CEO's line that "customer satisfaction is visible on the operating margin".
  • Evidence against: on-time delivery at 91.1 percent in Q3 2026 against a 98 percent target; quality 99.6 percent against 100; late-2025 data-centre transformer defects and Bulgarian design-defect provisions; the 2023 loss of a SEK 250m battery-system account over quality; no expert-network evidence on AQ's site performance is in the files.
  • Net: utility is high and the customer behaviour proves it, but the current delivery slippage is precisely the dimension on which a customer would switch at re-sourcing; if it persists beyond two to three quarters it erodes the "not inferior" verdict.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

❌ D38

Judgement: little demonstrable pricing power. The evidence of application is of cost pass-through (with a lag), not of real price increases; management states that pricing ran at or below inflation in 2026; OEMs extract annual productivity cuts. Pricing power in data-centre magnetics is plausible but unapplied and unproven.

  • What AQ can do: pass metal and currency cost changes through via contractual clauses within 3 to 6 months; the 2022-2023 sequence (margin dip, then catch-up) proves the mechanism works over two years.
  • What it cannot do: raise value-added prices in real terms in the base; automotive and heavy-vehicle customers are the hardest to raise prices with (Risks report); OEMs protecting about 26 percent aftermarket margins squeeze suppliers.
  • What would demonstrate power: stable or rising margins in data-centre products while copper rises and while capacity is added (not disclosed); any statement that data-centre price increases are being achieved (none).
  • Peer contrast: Michelin and Trelleborg (from the sector material) pass through fully and price ahead of cost; SFS sustains higher margins in custom cold-formed parts; AQ resembles a build-to-print supplier more than a designed-in pricer in most of its book.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

C58

Question asked To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

Judgement: AQ shares its scale and cost economies with customers de facto and culturally, which is a source of durability rather than a weakness. Its stated value ("We are simple and cost-effective, which means we are competitive") is a scale-economies-shared philosophy: low cost is used to win and keep business, not to extract price.

  • Evidence: pricing on a par with or below inflation in 2026 while margins rose (cost and utilisation gains retained partly, but not used to raise price); acceptance of OEM productivity cuts as a cost of doing business; local-for-local footprint that lowers customers' logistics and carbon costs; buying distressed plants and moving customers' volumes onto AQ's lower-cost Eastern European base (e.g. Time 24 overhead moved to the Eastern European footprint).
  • Why it matters: in a price-taking industry, sharing savings is how share is gained and relationships kept; it is consistent with the margin ceiling near 10 percent (gains are shared, not banked) and with the long record of share gains.
  • Limit: it is not stated as strategy in the Costco or Ryanair sense, and the company also wants a higher margin (the raised target); the balance has so far tilted toward retention of gains (EBIT margin from 6.4 percent in 2019 to about 10 percent), suggesting mix, not price, explains most of the margin expansion.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

C55

Judgement: a meaningful but minority share of AQ's improvement is mechanical; the majority still depends on heroic local execution (turnarounds, ramp-ups, quality fixes). The balance is shifting slowly toward mechanical forces as the mix moves to design-responsible products and as the acquisition engine compounds.

  • Mechanical forces at work: (a) industry consolidation toward advantaged players (79 to 89 percent in a decade); (b) customer regionalisation; (c) net cash compounding into cheap acquisitions bought at about 6 times EBITA in downturns; (d) mix drift toward inductive and data-centre products (inductive 6 to 25 percent of revenue 2018-2025); (e) Monitor ERP and standard processes rolled out to each acquired plant; (f) the low-cost footprint's relative advantage as Western costs rise.
  • Heroic elements: each acquisition needs a turnaround (Time 24 now; mdexx needed 10 percent personnel cuts); each ramp needs local managers (India and Mexico show the supply of such managers is thin); quality and delivery require constant intervention; the BF report's operator section says sustaining above-target returns "depends on continual 'heroic' turnaround execution of low-margin acquisitions rather than purely mechanical or structural forces".
  • Peer contrast: Bekaert explicitly "lacks mechanical gravitational pull" and needed 13 plant closures to lift margins; Comer claims mechanical system-integration advantages; AQ sits between them.

Company - Economic strength

C6011 questions
C-4970C-5064C-5163C-5240C-5362C-5470C-7562C-7666C-7752C-7862C-7940

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

AQ is a clean-earning, high-return, low-teens-to-mid-teens EPS compounder whose weak link is cash conversion. EPS has compounded at about 17 percent a year since 2019 on 10 percent revenue growth and a margin rise from 6.4 to 9.3 percent; returns on capital are 16 to 17 percent and higher on new capital; earnings carry no adjusted measures or capitalised costs; operating leverage is moderate on the way up (15 to 16 percent drop-through) and has historically been low on the way down because materials pass through and labour flexes; there is no debt.

The Consumer should carry four structural facts into the model. First, future EPS growth will track revenue more closely than in the past because the margin is near its 10 percent ceiling (consensus 10.4 percent EBIT for FY2028 already assumes continued mix gains), so 11 to 14 percent EPS growth is the realistic range, not 17. Second, tax steps up from 2027 (Pillar II; 18 percent toward 20 to 22 percent), worth 2 to 5 percent of EPS. Third, net working capital of about 30 percent of sales absorbs roughly a third of every krona of growth, so FCF conversion will run at 0.5 to 0.8 times net income while growth exceeds 10 percent, recovering only when growth slows, which makes cash counter-cyclical and the balance sheet self-hedging. Fourth, the balance sheet is an unused lever: the evidenced acquisition cadence (2 to 4 percent of revenue a year) leaves cash accumulating, so upside scenarios should come from capital deployment rather than from margin.

A basis warning before the numbers: the financials block mixes bases. History EBITDA is FactSet "operating", consensus EBITDA is brokers' "adjusted"; history "gross margin" (8.9 percent in FY2025) is a FactSet definition after production costs and is not comparable with consensus gross profit (about 51 percent of revenue, a value-added basis matching the company's 53.8 percent for 2025); EPS comes on four bases (reported basic SEK 7.38, brokers' SEK 7.46, FactSet adjusted SEK 7.57, Viewforth SEK 7.65 for FY2025). Consensus growth below is computed from the brokers'-basis FY2025 base, as the block requires; consensus rests on four visible brokers (one for FY2029).

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

C70

Judgement: AQ has compounded EPS at about 17 percent a year over six years on revenue growth of about 10 percent, and the durable rate from here is lower, about 11 to 14 percent a year, because the margin contribution that supplied roughly 40 percent of past EPS growth is close to exhausted at a 10 percent EBIT margin and tax is about to rise.

  • History: reported basic EPS SEK 2.85 (FY2019) to SEK 7.38 (FY2025), 17.2 percent a year (calculated); revenue SEK 5,113m to SEK 9,071m, 10.0 percent a year (calculated, including acquisitions); adjusted EBIT SEK 325m to SEK 841m, 17.2 percent a year; share count flat at about 91.5m; the company cites a 14 percent EPS CAGR over ten years and met its "double EPS in five years" goal from FY2021 to the twelve months to Q3 2026. Year by year the path is lumpy: plus 54 percent in FY2023 (price-led boom and margin catch-up), plus 5 percent in FY2024 and plus 1.5 percent in FY2025 (organic decline, then 2 percent).
  • Decomposition: of the 2.6-times rise in EBIT since 2019, revenue explains a factor of about 1.77 and margin (6.4 to 9.3 percent) about 1.45 (calculated). Future margin expansion is limited (ceiling near 10 percent, with consensus at 10.4 percent EBIT for FY2028), so future EPS growth will track revenue growth more closely.
  • Current run-rate: nine-month 2026 EPS SEK 6.38 against SEK 5.55, up 15 percent; Q3 up 33 percent (SEK 2.23 against SEK 1.67). Consensus (brokers' basis): FY2026 SEK 8.77 (plus 17.6 percent), FY2027 SEK 10.15 (plus 15.8 percent), FY2028 SEK 11.05 (plus 8.8 percent); 14.0 percent a year FY2025-28 on revenue growth of 10.4 percent a year and EBIT growth of 14.6 percent a year (calculated).
  • Durability: the earnings base proved durable through two downturns (EPS rose in FY2020 and FY2024); the growth rate is durable only as long as the structural slice keeps compounding and acquisitions resume at 2 to 4 percent of revenue a year. Headwinds to EPS growth from 2027: Pillar II (about 1.3 percent of EPS per point of tax), slower margin gains, interest income lost if cash is spent.
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

C64

Judgement: returns on new capital are high, but the volume of capital AQ can deploy at those returns is modest relative to its cash generation, so AQ is a high-return, moderate-reinvestment compounder. That combination supports a premium to Nordic contract manufacturers (about 10 to 13 times EV/EBIT) but not a hyper-growth multiple; on my judgement a fair 24-month-forward P/E in normal conditions is about 18 to 21 times, bracketing the stock's ten-year median of 19.7 times.

  • Organic reinvestment: incremental EBIT on organic volume has been about 15 percent of incremental revenue in 2026 (Q2 15.9 percent, Q3 15.0 percent, calculated), against incremental capital of roughly 0.30 SEK of net working capital plus a small fixed-asset need per SEK of revenue (capex runs at about 2.6 percent of sales, below depreciation of about 3.7 percent). That implies pre-tax returns on incremental organic capital above 30 percent (calculated, approximate) while capacity is available. The constraint is demand and capacity steps, not capital.
  • Capacity projects: SEK 60m defence capacity, SEK 23m in Hungary, USD 3.7m at Wytheville, Norlake at USD 13.5m enterprise value for up to SEK 300m of sales at full utilisation: small sums with high returns if filled.
  • Acquisitions: at about 6 times EBITA the entry pre-tax return is about 17 percent, rising after AQ fills the plant (mdexx bought loss-making, now 7 percent EBT, targeted at up to 15 percent). Pool of targets is large; discipline (no deals in 2025) limits volume. Evidenced cadence 2 to 4 percent of revenue a year (acquisitions SEK 84m, 96m and 126m year-to-date in 2024, 2025, 2026 plus assumed debt).
  • Reinvestment rate: capex plus acquisitions plus working capital absorb most of free cash in growth years, but in normal years cash accumulates (net cash rose from SEK 591m to SEK 817m excluding leases in the year to Q2 2026), which signals that the reinvestment runway is smaller than cash generation. The memo puts the runway at about 2.6 percent of sales in capex plus 1 to 3 percent in M&A, far below the 20 percent reinvestment the framework associates with top compounders.
  • Mid- and long-term potential: 10 to 12 percent revenue growth and 11 to 14 percent EPS growth for three to five years is achievable; above that requires either a step-up in acquisitions (balance-sheet use) or the data-centre line becoming a much larger share.
C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

C63

Judgement: high and rising: after-tax ROIC 16.7 percent and pre-tax ROCE 16.9 percent in 2025 (from 11.9 percent ROIC in 2019); returns on new organic capital are well above that; returns on tangible capital are higher still because acquisitions are bought cheaply with little goodwill. Reported returns are depressed by two choices: holding about SEK 0.8 to 0.9bn of low-yielding net cash and carrying net working capital of about 30 percent of sales.

  • Evidence: adjusted after-tax ROIC 16.7 percent (2025) and pre-tax ROCE 16.9 percent (BF report, best-in-class against Scanfil and Inission); DNB Carnegie forecasts adjusted ROCE of 16.3 percent and ROE of 15.6 percent for 2027 (Addtech's ROE 33.6 percent with 0.5x leverage for contrast). The memo's tracking trigger is ROCE below 14 percent as a sign of deteriorating allocation.
  • Return on tangible capital: not disclosed; acquired intangibles are amortised over five to ten years and purchase prices are low (mdexx SEK 144m; Time 24 £1 plus debt; Norlake mostly assumed liabilities and property), so goodwill is a small share of capital and tangible returns exceed the reported ROIC.
  • Return on new invested capital: on organic growth above 30 percent pre-tax (C-50, calculated, approximate); on acquisitions about 17 percent pre-tax at entry rising with turnaround.
  • Peer view: Bekaert's underlying ROCE 14 to 28 percent by division with reported 6.4 percent; Comer high-teens ROIC; SFS 21.1 percent ROCE. AQ is in the upper half of the sector peers on returns and at the top of contract manufacturers.
C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

❌ D40

Judgement: conversion is the weak link in AQ's economics: about 83 percent of net income over seven years on FactSet's generous definition, highly volatile, and structurally low whenever revenue grows fast, because net working capital runs at about 30 percent of sales and is the main use of cash in growth years. For the next eighteen months the modeller should expect conversion well below 100 percent (consensus 0.5 times for FY2026 and 0.7 times for FY2027).

  • History (FactSet FCF = operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex; before acquisitions and lease payments): FY2019 96 percent of net income, FY2020 157, FY2021 13, FY2022 minus 34, FY2023 106, FY2024 134, FY2025 80; seven-year cumulative SEK 2,757m against net income of SEK 3,325m, 83 percent (calculated). After lease payments and acquisitions the true conversion is lower.
  • The mechanism, counter-cyclical: cash is absorbed in upswings (2021-22: days sales outstanding from 60 to 90; inventory from SEK 816m in 2020 to SEK 1,629m in 2022) and released in downturns (FY2024 operating cash flow SEK 1,197m). This makes the balance sheet self-hedging: AQ accumulates cash exactly when acquisition targets are cheapest.
  • Now: net working capital SEK 3,131m at Q3 2026, 31.9 percent of trailing revenue (28.7 percent a year earlier); it rose about SEK 580m while trailing revenue rose about SEK 950m, so about 61 percent of incremental revenue was absorbed in working capital over twelve months (calculated, including acquired businesses). Inventory turns 2.9 against a 3.5 target; SEK 185m of inventory write-downs in 2025. Operating cash flow nine months 2026 SEK 631m against SEK 798m; Q2 SEK 23m (late-quarter receivables SEK 219m, inventory SEK 70m), Q3 SEK 269m (still below Q3 2025's SEK 322m).
  • Consensus: operating cash flow SEK 858m, 964m, 1,260m and FCF SEK 406m, 648m, 866m for FY2026-28, i.e. 0.51, 0.70 and 0.86 times net income (calculated); implied Q4 2026 operating cash flow about SEK 227m. These assumptions are reasonable, even cautious for 2028, if growth slows toward 7 to 8 percent.
  • Peer contrast: Bekaert runs operating working capital at 15 percent of sales; Comer at 21.8 percent; SFS inventories 28.1 percent of sales; AQ's about 30 percent is the heaviest in the set, which is why AQ's FCF/net income in the peer table is the lowest of the five for C+1 (0.5x).
  • Modelling rule: net working capital at 30 to 32 percent of revenue, with an improvement case toward 28 percent if inventory turns move toward 3.5 (Monitor ERP, the 20 percent inventory-turn weight in executive bonuses). Each SEK 1bn of revenue growth absorbs about SEK 300m of cash.
C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

C62

Judgement: gross (value-added) margin is high and stable at 51 to 54 percent, operating margin is at a record of about 10 percent and has risen steadily for five years, and the trend from here is flat-to-modestly-up rather than steeply up.

  • Gross (value-added, company and brokers' basis): 53.0 percent (2024), 53.8 percent (2025); consensus gross profit implies 50.9, 51.3 and 51.8 percent for FY2026-28 (calculated), consistent with higher copper prices inflating revenue through pass-through rather than with any loss of value added. The CEO says gross margins have not improved structurally; margin gains come from overheads and fixed-cost absorption.
  • EBIT (FactSet adjusted): 6.4 (FY2019), 6.7, 7.1, 6.3, 8.8, 9.6, 9.3 percent (FY2025); quarterly 9.9 percent (Q2 2026) and 10.1 percent (Q3 2026), nine months 9.85 percent. Q3 is normally the seasonal low (European summer shutdowns; Q3 2025 was 9.0 percent); Q3 2026's 10.1 percent was helped by high utilisation in the US and China, which management and DNB Carnegie both call partly temporary. EBT margin above 8 percent for 14 quarters; 10.0 percent in Q3 2026.
  • EBITDA (brokers' basis): 12.9 percent (FY2025); consensus 13.4, 13.7, 13.9 percent. AQ's EBITDA margin is lower than sector peers' (15 to 20 percent) because it is less capital intensive; on EBIT it sits mid-pack against the sector peers (Daetwyler and SFS about 13 percent consensus, Bekaert 8 percent) and at the top of contract manufacturers (7 to 8 percent norm; Note 7.7 percent, Incap 8.8 percent in Q2 2026).
  • Consensus EBIT margin: 9.8 (FY2026), 10.1 (FY2027), 10.4 percent (FY2028), slightly above the "ceiling" both bull and bear research name; achievable only with continued mix shift.
C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

C70

Judgement: the earnings base is unusually clean for a serial acquirer; the material leakage ahead is tax.

  • Clean: the company reports no adjusted profit measures; zero capitalised R&D acquired customer relations and technology amortised over conservative five-to-ten-year lives (expensed above EBIT); brokers'-basis EPS SEK 7.46 against reported SEK 7.38 (1 percent gap); related-party dealings at market rates; unqualified EY audits.
  • Costs inside the base that a reader might call one-offs: SEK 185m of inventory write-downs in 2025 (the Risks report says recognised during the year; if all of it went through the 2025 income statement it equals about 22 percent of FY2025 EBIT, calculated, and the files do not confirm the treatment), quality provisions in Q4 2025, mdexx dilution in 2025. Because AQ never adjusts them out, they recur in the history; in an inventory-heavy model some write-down is a normal cost, so I would not normalise it upward.
  • Flattering items: small: a SEK 22m capital gain on the Gävle property in 2026; favourable US/China utilisation in Q3 2026.
  • Leakage: tax rate 18.1 percent (FY2025), 18.7 percent (FY2024), versus Pillar II top-up payments from 2027 because of low-tax Eastern European profits; consensus about 20 percent; the memo 22 percent by FY2028; the memo's alarm level 23 percent. Each point is about 1.3 percent of EPS. Minorities SEK 22m (small). Dilution: 2024/27 warrants at SEK 152.10 are in the money (exercise adds a fraction of a percent), the 2026/29 programme (up to 775,000 warrants at SEK 273) is out of the money; each programme under 1 percent dilution. Net financial items negligible (about SEK minus 2 to minus 5m a quarter).
  • Disclosure noise: the Q3 2026 interim report and investor presentation gave different prior-year Q3 EPS figures (SEK 1.67 against SEK 2.06); FactSet shows SEK 1.68.
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

C62

Judgement: operating leverage is moderate on the way up and historically low on the way down, an unusually favourable asymmetry for a manufacturer, explained by the business model: about half of revenue is pass-through material, labour sits mostly in flexible low-cost plants, and overheads are lean. The range of outcomes for 2027 EBIT is therefore moderate (about plus or minus 10 percent around consensus in ordinary scenarios), with a fatter tail only in a deep European volume recession.

  • Upside leverage: incremental EBIT margin on 2026 growth 15 to 16 percent (Q2 15.9, Q3 15.0, nine months 15.3 percent, calculated) against an average margin near 10 percent: each 1 percent of organic revenue moves EBIT by about 1.5 percent. Consensus embeds incremental margins of 14.3 percent (FY2026), 12.6 percent (FY2027) and 13.7 percent (FY2028) (calculated), slightly below the observed rate, which is sensible given dilutive acquired revenue.
  • Downside evidence: FY2020, revenue minus 5.7 percent, EBIT flat (SEK 325m); FY2024, revenue minus 4.6 percent with organic minus 6.9 percent, EBIT up 4.7 percent and margin up from 8.8 to 9.6 percent; Q2 2020 stayed profitable through a 21 percent organic fall. Partly explained by metal deflation shrinking revenue without shrinking value added, and by decentralised managers cutting capacity fast.
  • Where fixed-cost leverage does bite: local plants with specific customers (Mexico and Plattsburgh wiring, underutilised since bus volumes fell; India); a 50-basis-point margin change is about SEK 45m of EBIT on 2025 figures; the input-cost lag (FY2022 incremental margin of 3.7 percent).
  • Range for FY2027 (calculated, illustrative): a 5 percent organic swing in the base (about SEK 9.5bn of revenue) at 15 percent drop-through moves EBIT by about SEK 70m, about 6 percent of the SEK 1,150m consensus; a 20 percent swing in data-centre revenue (about SEK 0.9bn) at a higher drop-through moves EBIT by about SEK 35 to 45m. Combined adverse case about minus 10 percent; the Risks report's recession case (2027 sales SEK 9,000m, EBIT margin 8 percent, EBIT SEK 720m, about minus 37 percent against consensus) is the tail, and I judge an 8 percent margin as the realistic floor in a deep downturn (the old target, never breached in 14 quarters).
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

C66

Judgement: there is no financial leverage; it neither amplifies EPS nor adds risk, and it leaves an unused EPS lever.

  • Position: net cash SEK 817m excluding leases (Q2 2026); company-reported net cash SEK 467m including leases (Q3 2026); FactSet total debt SEK 436m including leases against cash SEK 904m; net debt to EBITDA minus 0.38x; equity ratio 67 to 68 percent (target above 40 percent); DNB Carnegie forecasts minus 0.2x for 2027.
  • EPS effect: net financial items about SEK minus 10m a year; a 1 percentage-point change in interest rates moves profit by about SEK 4m (2025 annual report). Spending the cash on acquisitions would remove a small interest income and add acquired EBITA at about 17 percent pre-tax yield, a net positive.
  • Comfort: very high; no covenants or refinancing risk; leases are the only debt-like item. The risk is the opposite (capital idle, dilutes ROE), which matters for the multiple and for the M&A question rather than for risk.
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C52

Judgement: the cost base is lean and largely variable already; little is cuttable in an upswing, but a large part flexes in a downturn, which is the source of AQ's margin resilience.

  • Materials (about 48 percent of revenue; Q1 2026 SEK 1,126m on revenue of SEK 2,358m): not cuttable as such; passed through to customers with a 3-to-6-month lag; reducible only through procurement (central purchasing, synergies in acquired units) by perhaps a point or two of material cost.
  • Personnel (the next largest block; about 7,848 staff, 65 percent in Eastern Europe): largely variable in low-cost plants (decentralised managers adjust hours and headcount fast; 20 percent annual turnover gives natural attrition); proven cuttable in acquisitions (mdexx personnel costs down 10 percent to reach break-even). Swedish and Western plants are less flexible.
  • Overheads: lean by culture (a small head office, more than 40 operating companies running themselves); little fat; the CEO says margin gains come from overhead discipline and low fixed costs.
  • Depreciation (about 3.5 to 3.7 percent of sales) and site fixed costs: not cuttable short-term; the drag from underutilised Mexico and Plattsburgh plants is the clearest reducible fixed cost (by filling or, if necessary, closing).
  • Quality and delivery costs: express freight, overtime and temporary staff rise whenever delivery precision slips (2017, 2022 precedents); recovering delivery precision from 91 percent would remove a cost currently in the base.
C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

C62

Judgement: there is plausible scope to lift EBIT margin to about 10.5 to 11 percent and to improve cash conversion materially over three years, from two big levers (mix shift to design-responsible inductive and data-centre products; filling new US capacity) and many small ones (site fixes, acquired-unit turnarounds, inventory discipline); it is congruent with the strategy, and peer precedents show margin gains of 1 to 3 points from self-help are achievable in this sector.

  • Big levers: (1) mix: the structural slice from about 12 percent of sales toward about 20 percent by FY2028 on the memo's path, lifting its share of group profit from roughly 16 to roughly 27 percent (memo estimate; segment margins undisclosed); (2) US capacity: Norlake (up to about SEK 300m of sales) plus Virginia (USD 50m capacity) filled with backlogged volumes, each new site arriving below group margin and converging.
  • Small levers: Time 24 to group margins (SEK 124m of sales); mdexx toward 15 percent; India quality and productivity; Mexico and Plattsburgh utilisation; Monitor ERP across acquisitions; automation; energy (solar); delivery precision recovery reducing expediting costs; inventory turns from 2.9 toward 3.5 (cash rather than margin; on the 2022 inventory base of SEK 1.6bn a 17 percent reduction would be of the order of SEK 250 to 350m, an estimate since the current inventory balance is not in the files).
  • Peer precedents: SFS targets plus 0.8 points of EBIT margin from network streamlining by 2027; Bekaert lifted underlying EBIT margin from 5.9 percent (2017-19) to 8.6 percent (2023-25) through closures; Daetwyler added 170 basis points from ForwardNow in a year; Comer expanded EBITDA margin from 11.1 to 16.7 percent in three years through Walterscheid synergies. AQ itself moved EBIT margin from 6.3 to 9.3 percent between FY2022 and FY2025.
  • Congruence: fully congruent with the stated strategy (design responsibility, complexity, capacity in growth niches, disciplined M&A) and with incentives (80 percent of bonus on EBT, 20 percent on inventory turns).
  • Caution: consensus already carries 10.4 percent EBIT for FY2028, so most of this scope is in the numbers; upside beyond consensus requires the data-centre line to outgrow consensus or acquisitions to accelerate.
C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter, how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

❌ D40

Question asked How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

Judgement: the perimeter is wide and widening, which lowers the predictability of any single line but smooths the group; for a modeller the practical consequence is that disclosure does not permit a bottom-up build by end market or product, so the model must be built at group level with an explicit structural-slice overlay.

  • Width: 17 countries; more than 40 operating companies in 24 divisions; seven product areas; two reporting segments (Component about 85 percent of sales; System about 15 percent; FactSet segment consensus Component SEK 9,312m, 10,373m, 11,044m and System SEK 1,430m, 1,559m, 1,644m with eliminations of about SEK minus 0.9bn); end markets spanning trucks, buses, construction and agricultural machinery, rail, defence, data centres, grid, medtech, semiconductors, packaging and marine; 73 percent of sales in foreign currencies.
  • Direction: widening in 2026 (UK semiconductors, US data-centre capacity, Swedish defence capacity); no exits.
  • Predictability: diversification produced 117 consecutive profitable quarters; but many independent moving parts (site issues in India, Mexico, New York, the UK; currency; metals) and no segment margins mean the market and the modeller cannot see the drivers, and surprises in both directions are frequent (Q3 2026 EBIT 16.7 percent above forecast). The memo scored the perimeter D for this reason.
