Delivery precision 91 to about 96 percent: less express freight, overtime, temporary labour +SEK 10 to 20m two to four quarters (2022-23 precedent) costs never quantified; medium-low
India inductive quality and productivity; Mexico and Plattsburgh utilisation +SEK 10 to 20m 2027-28, slow drag not quantified; leadership replaced in India; low
mdexx already in the base complete (Q3 2026) high

Why little reaches the line. OEMs demand 3 to 5 percent annual productivity cuts on renewals and AQ prices at or below inflation, so continuous productivity mostly pays for price, not margin. Eastern European wages rise faster than productivity (Polish corporate wages up 10 percent in July 2024, ING). Acquired units at 3 percent of sales a year add a fresh dilution each year. The net is consistent with my EBIT margin path of 9.65 percent underlying (2026) to 10.0 (2027) and 10.2 percent (2028).

How AQ differs from the peer programmes, and why that matters for the next Analyst. SFS (CHF 75m of one-off costs, 80 basis points by end-2027, about 650 jobs), Bekaert (EUR 162m of charges in FY2025, EBITu margin held at 8.0 percent), Daetwyler (CHF 37.9m charge, CHF 24m a year from 2028, EBIT margin 13 percent in H1 2026) and HANZA (SEK 66m Horizon charge) all booked visible one-offs and promised dated savings. AQ reports no adjusted items and fixes sites continuously inside reported EBIT, so its restructuring costs are already in the base and its savings arrive as a gradual tailwind with no kitchen-sink event and no dated promise. For predictability this is double-edged: there is nothing to miss, but there is also no clean, monitorable milestone; the only trackable proxies are delivery precision, inventory turns and Time 24's quarterly result.

Timing on the lines: 2027 sees Time 24 and delivery-precision gains (about 0.15 to 0.2 points); 2028 sees Norlake and India (about 0.15 points); beyond 2028 the fixes are offset by new acquisitions. Working capital: 31.0 percent of sales at end-2026, 30.8 percent (2027), 30.3 percent (2028), 30.0 percent thereafter, against 29.3 percent at end-2025; the 28 percent improvement case needs the 3.5-turn target met, which has not happened since it was set.

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

❌ D42

Judgement: price will add about 1 to 2 percent a year to nominal revenue (metal, energy and wage pass-through net of OEM productivity cuts) and approximately nothing to the margin in the base case; the one place where real price was available, US medium-voltage data-centre transformers in 2026, is being captured by peers and probably by ABB rather than by AQ, and the window to capture it closes as competitor capacity lands from late 2027. I model price as zero in real terms, with a risk of minus 0.1 to 0.2 points of margin in data-centre products from 2028.

What the evidence shows (dated):

  • AQ itself: the CEO said on the Q2 2026 call (14 July 2026) that price was a very small part of 9.9 percent growth, on a par with or slightly below inflation; nothing after Q3 changes that. AQ renegotiates at least annually; in 2023 it sought 3 to 5 percent increases on selected customers and recovered margin (EBT 6.8 percent in 2022 to 8.4 percent in 2023). In Q4 2021 the CEO admitted it had been too slow to pass on costs; in 2022 it halted some increases with automotive accounts.
  • The pass-through is smaller at group level than the first set implied. The 2025 annual report puts the metal content of AQ's semi-finished purchases at SEK 267.4m, of which copper SEK 214.6m (2,194 tonnes), about 2.4 percent of sales. A 10 percent copper move is therefore about SEK 21m of revenue and cost (0.2 percent of sales). This means (a) metal pass-through cannot explain the fall in consensus value-added gross margin from 53.8 to about 51 percent (that is about SEK 280m on FY2026 revenue); mix toward material-heavy transformers, defence heavy mechanics and bought-in systems must explain most of it; and (b) a copper or steel spike costs at most 0.2 to 0.4 points of margin for one to two quarters. I disagree with C-05 and C-53 on this point: the metal lag is a small, temporary margin item; mix is the real driver of the gross-margin line.
  • Data centres, the market is pricing up but AQ is not saying so. Hammond Power Solutions' gross margin rose from 29.2 percent (Q4 2025) to 31.5 percent (Q2 2026) "on price increases and improved factory absorption"; Tamura raised FY2027 guidance (August 2026) on data-centre PDU transformer demand "and price increases"; Jefferies (16 January 2026) puts the custom-product premium at mid-to-low single digits. AQ is "winning every US quote" (DNB Carnegie) and yet describes no price gain. The most likely reason is structure: AQ sells through ABB (about half of data-centre sales in Q2 2026, by the CEO), on multi-year agreements (a EUR 10m three-year take-or-pay contract in early 2025), so the scarcity rent is earned by ABB and taken by AQ as volume and utilisation. The CEO said on the Q3 call that data-centre sales are now spread across several customers; that may open repricing at renewals in 2027-28.
  • The window closes in 2027-29: Hammond adds about CAD 250m of annual capacity at Fort Worth from late 2027; Tamura doubled Tijuana capacity and bought 80 percent of EMG Power Electric (medium-voltage cast resin, consolidated 1 October 2026); Scanfil raised its growth target to 15 percent; ABB's CEO noted (16 July 2026) that hyperscalers wield immense price leverage. The Industry digest's rule applies: "the slot closes when new capacity lands in 2027 to 2029".

Contribution to the lines (base case):

FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029-30
Price in revenue growth about +1% about +1.5% about +1% about +1%
Real price (above cost inflation) about 0 about 0 0 to slightly negative about 0
Margin effect of price about 0 0 to +0.05 pts 0 to -0.1 pts about 0

Upside case (not in base): AQ reprices data-centre contracts at 2027 renewals by 3 to 5 percent on about SEK 1bn of revenue, worth SEK 30 to 50m of EBIT (0.3 to 0.4 points). Downside case: a capacity overhang in North American dry-type transformers in 2028-29 plus the 800V re-award forces 3 to 5 percent price give-backs on the data-centre line, costing a similar amount. Price is therefore a symmetric, data-centre-specific swing factor of about plus or minus 0.3 points from 2028, not a driver of the base.

P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C60

Question asked After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: in 2027 the reinforcing data-centre capacity-and-design-win loop dominates revenue, while balancing loops dominate cash and cap the margin; in 2028 the balancing loops in data centres (competitor capacity, re-award, customer leverage) start to bind, and the swing factor becomes the counter-cyclical acquisition loop, which runs weakly in an upswing and strongly in a downturn. The net shape on the lines is: revenue growth decelerating gracefully (12.8, 10.0, 8.0 percent), margin plateauing near 10 to 10.3 percent, and cash conversion rising as growth slows (FCF after leases at 0.61, 0.70, 0.85 times net income in FY2026-28). The most important insight is that the loops hedge each other: whatever stalls the data-centre loop (a capex pause, a recession) also restarts the acquisition loop by releasing working capital and cheapening targets.

The loops, ranked by their effect on the lines:

  1. Reinforcing: data-centre capacity and design-win loop (strong through 2027, weakening 2028). Qualified slots lead to capacity (70 systems a month in Q2 2026; Norlake doubling US inductive capacity, 21 September 2026; further lines in 2027), which leads to more wins and learning. Evidence it is still strengthening: ABB's electrification orders reached USD 7.7bn in Q3 2026, up 70 percent organically with data-centre orders up triple digits (20 October 2026). What stops it: capacity and people first (Virginia full at about USD 50m; Norlake at USD 7m must reach USD 35m under the same manager), then competitor capacity (Hammond, Tamura from late 2027), then the 800V DC and solid-state transformer re-award (ABB's 800V solid-state product expected H2 2027; Jefferies, 23 October 2026: hybrid sidecar DC first, native solid-state by 2031, "timing slips as pilots become the test"). Line effect: data-centre revenue about +50 percent in 2027, +20 percent in 2028, +10 percent a year after (my base), adding about 3.2, 1.8 and 1.0 points to group growth.
  2. Balancing: working-capital absorption (strong now, reversing from 2028). Net working capital at 31.9 percent of trailing sales (Q3 2026) against 28.7 percent a year earlier; nine-month operating cash flow SEK 631m against 798m; Q2 2026 SEK 23m. Volex shows the same loop (cash conversion 63.9 percent, inventory up 36 percent in FY2026). Line effect: FCF conversion stays below 0.75 times net income until growth falls below about 8 percent; then it releases, which feeds loop 4.
  3. Balancing: delivery and quality strain (live, medium). Delivery precision 91.1 percent (Q3 2026) against 98 percent; late-2025 transformer redesign; the 2023 loss of a SEK 250m battery account over quality is the precedent for what this loop can do at a re-award. Line effect in the base: expediting costs inside margin for two to four more quarters; in the downside, a lost programme.
  4. Reinforcing, counter-cyclical: acquire cheap capacity and fill it (weak now, strong in a downturn). It worked in 2024 (five deals as organic growth fell), stalled in 2025 (multiples too high), restarted cheaply in 2026 (Time 24 for £1 plus SEK 50m of debt, Gumboda SEK 2m, Norlake SEK 133m including real estate). At about 6 times EBITA the return is about 16.7 percent before turnaround. Line effect: about 3 percent acquired growth a year in my base, at diluting then converging margins.
  5. Balancing: customer and channel leverage (structural). OEM productivity cuts; ABB as both largest data-centre customer and route to hyperscalers; ABB's own customers squeezing it on price. Line effect: caps real price at zero (P-04).
  6. Reinforcing: nearshoring and local-for-local (slow, steady). Adds share in the base at the rate of programme awards; supports the base's 2.5 to 3 percent organic growth through the cycle.
  7. Balancing: management bandwidth (live). Dan Day runs Virginia and Norlake; Rockford staff run Time 24; India leadership replaced. This loop governs how fast loops 1 and 4 convert into margin and is why I model 12 to 24 months for new capacity to reach group margins.

Which dominates and when (the judgement the next Analyst needs): 2027: loop 1 dominates revenue (still capacity-bound, demand above supply), loops 2, 3 and 7 dominate cash and margin quality; net, revenue is robust and EBIT is near consensus but FCF is the line most exposed. 2028: loops 1 and 5 meet; data-centre growth halves and pricing turns neutral to negative; the base cycle decides whether loop 4 restarts. The configuration is unusual and favourable for downside protection: a hyperscaler capex pause or European recession would cut revenue and EBIT, but would release SEK 300 to 500m of working capital (FY2024 precedent: operating cash flow SEK 1,197m) into a cheaper acquisition market. The configuration is unfavourable for upside surprise in 2028-29, because the strongest reinforcing loop meets the strongest balancing loops at the same time.

P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

C60

Judgement: the record justifies high weight on the margin and EBIT forecasts for the next 18 months, medium weight on revenue, low weight on free cash flow and on anything beyond 2028; the lean is up on EBIT for the next two to three quarters, down on acquired growth, working capital and the long-dated revenue ambitions, and up on the tax rate. Applied to my base case, the record says: do not chase the Q3 2026 beat into FY2027, but do not cut near-term EBIT either.

The record by line (dated):

Why weight differs so much by line. AQ controls cost and margin with a decentralised profit-and-loss culture; it does not control the cycle, seller multiples or customer payment terms. The record reflects exactly that split. Its refusal to give near-term guidance means the beat pattern partly reflects cautious sell-side modelling, so the near-term upward lean is real but modest: I place Q4 2026 EBIT at SEK 270m against an implied consensus of SEK 267m, not higher, because Q4 also carries Time 24 losses, Norlake start-up and expediting costs at 91 percent delivery precision.

Weight by horizon. Next 18 months: EBIT forecasts deserve high weight (the margin floor and the structural slice's visibility are evidenced); the cone of FY2027 EPS is about minus 15 to plus 9 percent around my base (P-10). FY2028: medium weight; the base business cycle and the data-centre capacity race both resolve in this year. FY2029-30: low weight; they depend on the 800V DC and solid-state transformer re-award, on which there is no evidence yet, and on whether management uses the balance sheet.

Direction against consensus. Lean consensus FY2027-28 EPS down by 3 to 4 percent, mainly on tax (P-08) and slightly lower margin, while accepting that Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 prints are more likely to beat than miss. Lean consensus FCF neither way for 2026-27 (consensus is already cautious), but note that consensus FCF lines do not reconcile with its own operating cash flow less capex (FY2026: 858 less 264 is 594, against 406), so FCF consensus is a weak anchor.

P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

❌ D42

Judgement: below-EBIT items turn from a tailwind into a headwind; over 2020-23 a tax rate of 14 to 16 percent lifted EPS, while over 2026-30 a rate rising to about 21.5 to 22 percent takes about 7 percent off net income against that era, and none of interest, one-offs, minorities or share count offsets it. The net effect is that EPS grows about 1 point a year slower than EBT from 2026 to 2028, and FY2027 free cash flow takes a one-year cash-tax step that is larger than the P&L step. I partly disagree with the first set on timing: the P&L step has probably already started, and 2027 is mainly a cash event.

Tax, the item that matters (base case: 19.8, 20.8, 21.3, 21.5, 21.8 percent for FY2026-30):

  • History: 17.5 percent (FY2019), 13.8, 16.2, 14.5, 15.4, 18.7, 18.1 percent (FY2025) on FactSet's basis; the low years reflect untaxed retained profit in Estonia and 10 percent tax in Bulgaria.
  • The P&L rate has already stepped up: on company EBT and reported net income (minorities about SEK 1 to 2m a quarter) the rate was about 17.4 percent in nine months 2025, about 20 percent in Q4 2025 and about 19.8 percent in nine months 2026 (calculated). Pillar II has applied to AQ since 1 January 2025 and top-up tax is a current-year expense under the accounting rules even though cash is paid later; the 2025 annual report says Bulgaria, Estonia and Lithuania failed the transitional safe harbours. The step from Q4 2025 is consistent with top-up being accrued. Confidence: medium (the files do not state the accrual).
  • Therefore 2027 is mainly a cash step: DNB Carnegie models cash tax rising from SEK 138m (2026) to SEK 218m (2027) while holding the P&L rate at 20 percent; that pattern (cash up about 60 percent, P&L flat) is what paying accrued top-up looks like. I model cash tax of about SEK 150m (2026) and 255m (2027), against P&L tax of about 199m and 233m.
  • A further, slower P&L creep: the substance-based carve-outs (payroll and tangible assets) that shield much of Bulgarian and Estonian profit decline each year toward 2033, and Bulgarian profit grows (SEK 1,308m of revenue in H1 2026; the three jurisdictions SEK 3,054m, about 62 percent of group sales). Bulgaria's enacted safe-harbour amendments (KPMG, 1 April 2025) are a mitigant. Hence about plus 0.5 points a year in 2027-28, then slower.
  • Against consensus: consensus implies 20.3, 19.7 and 20.2 percent (FY2026-28), i.e. it assumes the rate falls in 2027 below the 2026 run-rate. That is the least defensible line in consensus: each point is about 1.3 percent of EPS, so consensus FY2027-28 EPS is about 1.4 percent too high on tax alone. The first set's "1 to 2 points too low" is right on direction; I put it at the lower end because the step has already partly happened.

Interest: net financial items were SEK minus 9m in FY2025 on the company basis and about minus 3m in nine months 2026 (FactSet's "net interest expense" of minus 32m is gross interest including about SEK 400m of leases). With cash compounding, I model minus 6, minus 4, plus 2, plus 10 and plus 18 (FY2026-30); immaterial to EPS (under 0.2 SEK a share) unless the cash is deployed (P-02).

One-offs: AQ reports none, so none are modelled; but the base carries recurring items a reader might call one-offs: SEK 185m of inventory write-downs recognised in 2025, quality provisions in Q4 2025, SEK 10m local write-offs, and a SEK 22m Gävle property gain in 2026. The Gävle gain flatters FY2026 EPS by about SEK 0.19 (calculated); the write-downs are a normal cost of an inventory-heavy model and I do not add them back.

Minorities and associates: minorities about SEK 5 to 7m a year (about 0.6 percent of net income); no associates. The model's EPS will be about 0.7 percent above my cross-check because it does not deduct minorities.

Share count, buybacks and issuance: no buybacks ever and none modelled. Warrant exercises add shares for cash: 2024/27 programme 67,500 shares at SEK 152.10 (about SEK 10m, 2027); 2026/29 programme 90,500 subscribed of up to 775,000, at SEK 273 (about SEK 25m in 2029 if in the money). Dilution under 0.2 percent a year; share count 91.7m rising to about 91.95m by 2030.

Effect on the three lines (base case, calculated): EBT grows about 14.3 percent a year FY2025-28 on the company basis (SEK 831m to 1,242m), while net income on the brokers' basis grows about 12.8 percent a year, rising from about SEK 684m (FY2025, 7.46 times 91.7m) to 807m, 891m, 983m (FY2026-28); EPS 8.80, 9.71, 10.70. FCF after leases: SEK 495m, 620m, 835m; the FY2027 cash-tax step costs about SEK 20 to 40m (0.2 to 0.4 SEK a share) above the P&L charge in that year.

P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C56

Judgement: AQ moves from a period in which margin, tax and a price-led boom amplified revenue growth into EPS (revenue 10.0 percent a year, EPS 17.2 percent, FY2019-25) into one in which almost nothing amplifies it (revenue about 9 percent a year, EPS about 11 percent, FY2025-30), with cash conversion improving as growth slows. The compounding engine is intact; the gearing between revenue and EPS has largely gone.

The net of the set's items, in order of size (effect on FY2028 EPS against a world where 2026's margin and tax simply continued): mix and capacity fill (+3 to 4 percent, P-01, P-05); site fixes (+2 to 3 percent, P-03); acquired dilution (minus 1 percent, P-01); price (about 0, P-04); tax (minus 2 to 3 percent, P-08); interest (+0.5 percent, P-02). Net about +2 to 3 percent; that is why my EBIT margin rises only 0.55 points over 2026-28 and EPS grows at about the rate of EBIT less the tax drag.

What is different from the last four to six years, and why it matters for the third and fourth sets:

  1. Margin is no longer the main EPS driver. It supplied about 40 percent of 2019-25 EPS growth (EBIT margin 6.4 to 9.3 percent); from here it supplies about 10 percent (9.65 underlying to 10.3 percent). EPS predictability therefore rests on revenue, which is the less predictable line.
  2. Revenue growth is more structural and less price-led than 2022-23, but more concentrated: the structural slice supplies about 65 percent of 2027 organic growth from 14 percent of sales; data centres alone supply about 43 percent.
  3. Tax goes from tailwind to headwind (P-08).
  4. Cash conversion improves as growth slows, the opposite of 2021-22, unless working-capital discipline fails again.
  5. The balance sheet becomes the main source of upside, not operations (P-02).

Versus consensus: revenue in line for FY2026 and about 1 percent lower for FY2027-28 (my organic 7.5 percent plus 3 percent acquired less 0.5 percent currency, against consensus's 11.2 percent with no unannounced acquisitions, which needs about 10 percent organic); EBIT in line for FY2026 and 1 to 2 percent lower for FY2027-28; EPS in line for FY2026 and 3 to 4 percent lower for FY2027-28. The gap is narrow on operations and concentrated below EBIT.

P-10

C57

It sits at consensus for FY2026, 3 to 4 percent below consensus EPS for FY2027-28, and below the company's EPS-doubling ambition unless the balance sheet is used.

Scenarios for FY2027 (probabilities are my judgement):

  • Bear, 20 percent: a European volume recession from mid-2027 plus a data-centre stall (hyperscaler financing or ABB redesign); revenue +3 percent, EBIT margin 9.0 percent, EPS about SEK 8.2 (minus 15 percent against base); FY2028 EPS about SEK 7.6 with an EBIT margin near the 8 to 8.5 percent floor; FCF rises as working capital releases.
  • Base, 60 percent: as above, EPS SEK 9.71.
  • Bull, 20 percent: data centres +70 percent, base +5 percent organic, acquired growth 4 percent, EBIT margin 10.5 percent; EPS about SEK 10.6 (+9 percent); FY2028 about SEK 12.0. Balance-sheet deployment would add up to 20 percent more by 2030 and is outside all three.
  • Probability-weighted FY2027 EPS about SEK 9.6. The EPS cone is skewed down because the bear case combines a cyclical and a structural shock that share one cause (financing conditions), while the bull case is capacity-capped.

What would move me off the base case: organic growth in Q1 2027 below 10 percent (bear) or above 15 percent with delivery precision above 95 percent (bull); any data-centre repricing or loss disclosed at the ABB account; the FY2026 annual report's tax note (Pillar II accrual, carve-outs); Norlake revenue run-rate by Q2 2027 (USD 15m-plus annualised is on track); a deal larger than SEK 500m of enterprise value (balance-sheet regime change).

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

AQ reports two segments, Component (about 85 percent of sales) and System (about 15 percent); the files hold segment revenue only, and no segment or product-area margins. FactSet history (external sales): Component SEK 7,727m and System SEK 1,344m in FY2025. FactSet's segment consensus (Component 9,312m, System 1,430m, group items minus 910m for FY2026) does not sum to its own group revenue consensus of 10,226m, so it should not be used as a cross-check.

My segment split (external sales, SEKm; estimates): Component 8,791 (FY2026), 9,694, 10,504, 11,306, 12,166 (FY2030); System 1,440, 1,560, 1,650, 1,760, 1,880. System growth comes from Time 24 (about SEK 124m a year, loss-making in Q3 2026), defence and battery-storage cabinets; Component carries almost all of the structural slice.

Because reported segments do not separate the drivers, the model rests on a structural-slice overlay (my estimates; margins undisclosed):

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue. FY2026: nine months SEK 7,501m plus Q4 2,730m (+17.5 percent: organic about 15 percent with delayed data-centre and Finnish defence deliveries recovered, acquired about 2.8 percent from Time 24, Gumboda and Norlake, currency about zero). FY2027 +10.0 percent = organic about 7.5 percent (data centres +50 percent, capacity-bound by Norlake fill and 2027 transformer lines; defence +25 percent; base business +3 percent as restocking plateaus and comparisons harden from Q2 2027) + acquired about 3.0 percent (carry-over of 2026 deals plus new bolt-ons at the evidenced 2 to 4 percent cadence) - currency about 0.5 percent (stronger krona against the dollar). FY2028 +8.0 percent (organic about 5.5 percent as data-centre growth halves on competitor capacity and the 800V re-award window; acquired 2.8 percent). FY2029-30 +7.5 percent (organic 4.5 percent through the cycle, acquired 3 percent). Q3 2026's 17.9 percent organic growth was mostly the base business recovering from a weak Q3 2025, so it is not extrapolated.
  • Quarterly path. Q1 2027 +16.2 percent is against a Q1 2026 depressed by minus 4.8 percent currency; growth drops to single digits from Q2 2027. Q3 2027 assumes a mild seasonal dip (Q3 2026 was flat on Q2 because of US and China utilisation during European holidays, which management called partly temporary).
  • EBITDA margin. Company basis (EBIT plus total D&A), matching the brokers' basis (FY2025 12.9 to 13.0 percent). FY2026 13.4 percent includes a SEK 22m Gävle property gain (about 0.2 points). The EBIT margin rises from about 9.65 percent underlying (2026) to 10.0, 10.2, 10.25 and 10.3 percent through mix toward the structural slice and the Time 24, Norlake, India and delivery-precision fixes, partly offset by about 3 percent a year of acquired revenue at below-group margins, OEM productivity price cuts and Eastern European wage inflation. Incremental EBIT margins about 11 to 13 percent, against 15 percent in nine months 2026. Real price contributes nothing in the base case.
  • D&A. Total D&A about 3.55 percent of sales (SEK 362m, 400m, 431m, 464m, 499m), rising with capex at 3.0 percent of sales in 2027 (northern Sweden defence capacity SEK 60m, Hungary, 2027 transformer lines, Norlake property) and with right-of-use depreciation. The inputs exclude my estimate of amortisation of acquired intangibles (SEK 10m in FY2026 rising to 18m in FY2030; not disclosed in the files), as the form requires.
  • Net financial items. About SEK minus 1 to minus 2m a quarter today (company EBT less EBIT); turning positive as net cash including leases compounds from about SEK 0.6bn (end-2026) to about 2.4bn (end-2030) at about 2 percent interest income, against lease interest on about SEK 400m of lease liabilities.
  • Tax. The P&L rate already rose to about 19.8 percent in nine months 2026 (calculated from company EBT and reported net income), consistent with Pillar II top-up being accrued since 2025 in Bulgaria, Estonia and Lithuania; further creep of about 0.5 points a year in 2027-28 as substance carve-outs decline and Bulgarian profits grow, then slower. Cash tax steps up more than P&L tax in 2027 (my estimate SEK 255m cash against 233m P&L) as accrued top-up is paid.
  • Dividend. About 24 percent of EPS, the realised payout under the policy of about 25 percent of cycle-average profit; declared for each year and paid the following April.
  • Share count. No buybacks, no scrip, no shares issued for acquisitions; warrant exercises (2024/27 at SEK 152.10, about SEK 10m in 2027; 2026/29 at SEK 273, about SEK 25m in 2029 if in the money) are entered as shares issued for cash. Basic shares 91.7m rising to about 91.95m.
  • Capex. SEK 280m in FY2026 (201m in nine months plus Q4 projects), then 3.0, 2.8, 2.7 and 2.7 percent of sales.
  • Working capital. 31.9 percent of trailing sales at Q3 2026; 31.0 percent at end-2026 after Q4 deliveries; easing slowly to 30 percent as growth slows. The 3.5 inventory-turn target is not assumed met (2.9 now; missed since it was set). Part of each year's increase is acquired working capital paid through the acquisitions line (about 25 percent of acquired revenue).
  • Lease payments. My estimate from about SEK 400m of lease liabilities (FactSet total debt SEK 436m, against the company's SEK 817m net cash excluding leases at Q2 2026), growing with sales; not disclosed in the files.
  • Acquisitions. SEK 130m in FY2026 (126m in nine months, including Time 24's debt redemption and Norlake); then about SEK 160 to 195m a year, buying about 3 percent of sales a year at about 0.45 times sales (about 6 times EBITA on sub-par margins). No balance-sheet step-up in the base case.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

AQ gives no numeric near-term guidance. Its long-term targets, and my base case against them:

  • EBT margin above 10 percent (raised from 8 percent on 14 July 2026): my EBT margin is 9.8 percent (FY2026, including the Gävle gain), 9.96 percent (FY2027), 10.2 percent (FY2028), 10.3 and 10.4 percent (FY2029-30). The target is met from FY2027-28, consistent with its character as a normal-conditions floor; no full year has yet exceeded 9.6 percent.
  • Sales growth above 15 percent a year (10 percent organic, 5 percent acquired): my growth is 12.8, 10.0, 8.0, 7.5 and 7.5 percent; organic exceeds 10 percent only in FY2026 and acquired growth runs at about 3 percent. The target was missed in 2024 and 2025 and I do not use it.
  • Double EPS every five years (next doubling by 2031): from reported EPS of SEK 8.22 in the twelve months to Q3 2026, doubling needs about SEK 16.4 by 2031; my path reaches about SEK 13.4 to 13.6 in FY2031 (extrapolated), a shortfall of about 17 percent that only balance-sheet deployment of about SEK 3bn at 6 times EBITA would close. The base case does not assume it.
  • Equity ratio above 40 percent: met throughout; the ratio rises above 70 percent.
  • Dividend of about 25 percent of cycle-average profit: my 24 percent payout matches.
  • Operational KPIs (98 percent delivery precision, 3.5 inventory turns, 100 percent quality): not assumed met; delivery precision recovers partially over two to four quarters.
  • Informal commentary (Q3 2026 call): data-centre demand ahead of management's expectations, Q4 backlog recovery, Norlake doubling US capacity, further capacity in 2027: reflected in data-centre growth of 50 percent in 2027 and Q4 2026 growth of 17.5 percent.

Against consensus (FactSet median, four brokers): revenue 10,231 / 11,254 / 12,154 against 10,226 / 11,367 / 12,216 (FY2026-28); reported EBIT 1,009 / 1,125 / 1,240 against 1,006 / 1,150 / 1,266; EPS (brokers' basis) 8.80 / 9.71 / 10.70 against 8.77 / 10.15 / 11.05. The FY2027-28 EPS gap of 3 to 4 percent is about 1.4 points tax (consensus implies 19.7 and 20.2 percent), about 1 point EBIT, and the rest minorities and consensus's own non-reconciling medians (FY2027 net income 920 over EPS 10.15 implies 90.6m shares against 91.7m in issue).

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Revenue: identical on all bases.
  • EBIT: the company's EBIT is after all depreciation and amortisation, including acquired intangibles; AQ reports no adjusted measures. Annual company EBIT equals FactSet's adjusted EBIT (FY2025: 840 against 841). FactSet's quarterly EBIT understates the company's (Q1-Q3 2026: 211, 245, 240 against company 225, 255, 259), so quarterly work should use company figures. The SEK 22m Gävle gain is inside 2026 reported figures; consensus may or may not strip it.
  • EBITDA: company EBIT plus total D&A (FY2025: 840 plus 337, about 1,177) equals FactSet operating EBITDA (1,178) and the brokers' basis (1,172) within 0.5 percent. My Part 1 and Part 3 margins are on this basis. FactSet's quarterly EBITDA line is understated in the same way as EBIT.
  • D&A in Part 1: total D&A less my estimate of acquired-intangible amortisation (about SEK 10 to 18m a year). The model's EBIT will therefore sit about SEK 10 to 18m above reported EBIT, which approximates the brokers' basis.
  • Pre-tax profit: company EBT (FY2025 SEK 831m) differs from FactSet's pre-tax line (SEK 800m); I use company EBT. Net financial items are company EBT less EBIT (FY2025 minus 9); FactSet's "net interest expense" (minus 32) is gross interest including leases and should not be used as net financial items.
  • Tax: P&L tax rate on company EBT; cash tax diverges in 2027 (accrued Pillar II top-up paid).
  • Net income and EPS: brokers' FY2025 EPS SEK 7.46 against reported basic SEK 7.38 (+1.1 percent, roughly after-tax acquired amortisation) and FactSet adjusted diluted SEK 7.57. My cross-check EPS is reported net income plus after-tax acquired-intangible amortisation, after minorities (about SEK 5 to 7m a year), over basic shares (91.7m to 91.95m); it includes the Gävle gain (about SEK 0.19 in FY2026). Reported EPS on my numbers: 8.71, 9.61, 10.58, 11.44, 12.39. The model's EPS will be about 0.7 percent above my cross-check because it does not deduct minorities.
  • Dividends: mine are declared for each fiscal year (paid the next April); FactSet's DPS history is dated by payment period (the SEK 1.80 declared for FY2025 was paid in April 2026).
  • Net cash: FactSet's debt includes about SEK 400m of leases (Q3 2026 net cash 468 including leases); the company also quotes net cash excluding leases (SEK 817m at Q2 2026).
  • Free cash flow: my FCF is operating cash flow less capex including intangibles and less lease payments, before acquisitions and dividends. FactSet's historical FCF deducts only fixed-asset capex; consensus FCF (406, 648, 866) does not reconcile with consensus operating cash flow less capex (594, 670, 947), so compare with care. My operating cash flow is about SEK 871m, 1,060m and 1,286m (FY2026-28) against consensus 858m, 964m and 1,260m.

Earnings per share on the brokers' adjusted basis (SEK), after minorities; free cash flow in SEKm after capex including intangibles and after lease payments, before acquisitions and dividends.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: AQ Group AB (AQ-SE, Nasdaq Stockholm Large Cap): a decentralised Swedish contract manufacturer of components and systems for industrial OEMs, with more than 40 operating companies in 17 countries; Component segment (inductive components and transformers including medium-voltage units for data-centre UPS systems, wiring harnesses, sheet metal, injection moulding) and System segment (electrical cabinets and complete system assembly), serving commercial vehicles, construction and agricultural machinery, rail, defence, grid and data-centre power customers.

Request: T-D1

Peers: Volex (data-centre cable and power assembler whose data-centre sales doubled in its FY2026 while cash conversion fell); Hammond Power Solutions (North American dry-type transformer maker scaling for data centres); Tamura (transformer and inductor maker expanding in Mexico and buying EMG Power Electric); Kitron (Nordic contract manufacturer whose growth is defence- and electrification-led); Scanfil (Nordic contract manufacturer that raised its growth target to 15 percent on 29 September 2026); HANZA (acquisitive Swedish contract manufacturer with a history of low margins); Note and Incap (contract manufacturers facing price competition); KB Components (Swedish roll-up that fills acquired plants); Lifco and Addtech (decentralised Swedish serial acquirers, for the acquisition engine); Halma (its Avo Photonics unit as a data-centre ramp inside a compounder); MGM Transformer and Forgent Power Solutions (North American medium-voltage transformer competitors). Operating conditions: the AI data-centre power build-out under tightening financing (hyperscaler and neocloud credit spreads widening in October 2026); the 800V DC and solid-state transformer transition of 2026 to 2030; a European heavy-truck and agricultural-machinery cycle with spare capacity in Eastern Europe; the electronics restocking pulse plateauing around Q4 2026; copper above USD 14,000 a tonne (May 2026) and EU steel out-of-quota duties of 50 percent from 1 July 2026; Eastern European wage inflation; European rearmament procurement timing; OECD Pillar II from 2025. Inputs: Can a contract manufacturer without its own intellectual property sustain a pre-tax margin above 10 percent (AQ raised its target from 8 to above 10 percent on 14 July 2026, after Q2 and Q3 2026 EBT margins of 9.9 and 10.0 percent, while no full year has exceeded 9.6 percent) once a capacity-constrained data-centre and defence ramp matures? How plausible is filling the Norlake plant in Cleveland (acquired 21 September 2026; USD 7m of 2025 sales; management cites up to USD 35m at full utilisation) under the same manager who runs the full Virginia plant, and how long have comparable US capacity ramps taken? What precedents exist for recovering delivery precision (AQ: 91.1 percent in Q3 2026 against a 98 percent target; 90 percent in 2022, later recovered) and inventory turns (2.9 against a 3.5 target) during a growth surge, and what did failure cost (AQ lost a SEK 250m battery-system account over quality in 2023)? How repeatable is the acquire-cheap-and-fill model (DNB Carnegie cites about 6 times EBITA; mdexx moved from about minus 7 to plus 7 percent EBT and reached group margins by Q3 2026; Time 24 bought for GBP 1 plus about SEK 50m of debt is behind plan), and how often have serial acquirers kept a 5 percent a year acquired-growth target when seller multiples rose (AQ made no deals in 2025)? Which strengths are demonstrated (117 consecutive profitable quarters; profit rose in the 2024 downturn) and which would have to be acquired (US scale in medium-voltage magnetics, solid-state transformer design wins, site leaders for multiple simultaneous turnarounds)? Focus: Whether AQ Group's decentralised, cost-led model can convert a capacity-constrained data-centre and defence ramp into a sustained pre-tax margin above 10 percent while restoring delivery precision, and how often comparable companies attempting the same shift from a similar starting point have succeeded, and how long it took.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Volex (data-centre growth with falling cash conversion and short contracts); Halma (Avo Photonics, a single-customer data-centre ramp valued below the core); Hammond Power Solutions and Tamura (transformer makers whose 2026 results rest on data-centre demand); Kitron and Scanfil (Nordic contract manufacturers whose 2027 forecasts depend on defence and electrification); Note, HANZA, Incap and Inission (Nordic contract manufacturers exposed to the European industrial cycle); SFS Group and Bekaert (European component makers that de-rated on European industrial weakness); Comer Industries (agricultural-machinery supplier whose revenue fell 23 percent in 2024 on destocking). Operating conditions: a hyperscaler capital-spending pause or financing squeeze; a synchronised European heavy-truck and industrial recession while metal prices stay high; the end of the electronics restocking pulse; competitor capacity additions in North American transformers from late 2027; the 800V DC design re-award window of 2026 to 2028; Swedish krona swings against the euro and dollar; rising bond yields (US 10-year near 5 percent in October 2026). Inputs: How concentrated are the drivers of AQ Group's 2027-28 results: the data-centre magnetics line (6 percent of group sales in Q2 2026; inductive components for electrification up more than 60 percent in Q3 2026; the CEO said on 14 July 2026 that one legacy electrification customer was about half of data-centre sales and on 15 October 2026 that sales were now spread across several customers), the base business (group organic growth of 17.9 percent in Q3 2026 against 2.0 percent in FY2025 and minus 6.9 percent in FY2024), acquired growth (2.2 percent in Q3 2026 against a 5 percent target), the tax rate under Pillar II (18.1 percent reported for FY2025; first additional payments expected in 2027), and working capital (operating cash flow SEK 23m in Q2 2026 against SEK 232m a year earlier)? How much of Q3 2026's growth and margin came from data centres, defence, base-business restocking, easy comparisons, US and China utilisation during European holidays (management's explanation) and one-off items such as the SEK 22m Gävle property gain reported in 2026? What is the historical dispersion of revenue and EBIT outcomes for contract manufacturers and component makers in the 18 months after a growth peak (2011, 2018-19, 2022-23 precedents), how often were forecasts resting on one data-centre customer or one technology ramp met (Volex, Halma's Avo, Hammond), and which events widened the range (destocking, customer redesign, financing shocks)? How did AQ's margins behave in 2020 and 2024 relative to revenue, and how much of that resilience came from metal-price deflation rather than cost flexibility? Focus: Whether AQ Group's 18-month outcome rests mainly on the data-centre ramp or on the European base-business cycle, how correlated the two are through financing conditions, and therefore how wide and how skewed the range of revenue, EBIT and free cash flow outcomes is through mid-2028.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Kitron, Scanfil, Note, HANZA, Incap and Inission (Nordic contract manufacturers with Q3 2026 results and guidance or targets); Volex and Huber+Suhner (data-centre component suppliers with recent trading updates); Hammond Power Solutions and Tamura (transformer makers that raised guidance or margins on data-centre demand in 2026); ABB (AQ's largest data-centre customer, Q3 2026 orders reported 20 October 2026); Trelleborg and SFS Group (Swedish and Swiss component makers with unhedged currency translation); Volvo Group (a major truck customer reporting 23 October 2026). Operating conditions: decelerating comparisons from Q1-Q2 2027 after Q3 2026 organic growth of 17.9 percent; recovery of delayed data-centre and Finnish defence deliveries in Q4 2026; the electronics restocking plateau; OECD Pillar II cash payments from 2027; the krona against the euro and dollar; copper and steel cost lags; hyperscaler capital-spending guidance in early 2027. Inputs: AQ Group gives no numeric near-term guidance; its targets are a pre-tax margin above 10 percent (raised 14 July 2026), sales growth above 15 percent a year (10 percent organic, 5 percent acquired; missed in 2024 and 2025), an equity ratio above 40 percent and doubling earnings per share every five years (next doubling by 2031). FactSet consensus at 25 October 2026 (four visible brokers, all Hold: DNB Carnegie, Nordea, Pareto and one restricted) is SEK 10,226m revenue and EPS SEK 8.77 for 2026, SEK 11,367m and SEK 10.15 for 2027, SEK 12,216m and SEK 11.05 for 2028, with FY2027 EPS up 7 percent in a month and 12 percent in six months. What revenue, margin, tax rate, acquired growth and working capital do the brokers assume for Q4 2026 and 2027-28, and do their models include unannounced acquisitions? How have AQ's results compared with consensus over the last twelve quarters (Q2 2026 EBIT about 6 percent above; Q3 2026 EBIT 16.7 percent above DNB Carnegie; Q4 2025 below on quality provisions)? What do peers' Q3 2026 results and outlooks imply for European truck, construction, defence, grid and data-centre demand into 2027? What would each outcome at the FY2026 report (11 February 2027) and the Q1 and Q2 2027 reports require, including the effect of harder comparisons, Pillar II tax and acquisitions? How likely is a further target revision or a capital markets day, and has management commented on the 2031 EPS ambition's dependence on acquisitions? Focus: Whether, over the next three to six reports, consensus earnings and the net breadth of revisions for AQ Group are more likely to rise (data-centre capacity, catch-up deliveries, acquisitions) or fall (harder comparisons from Q2 2027, the tax rate under Pillar II, the base-business cycle), and whether any target is likely to be raised, met or reduced.

Request: T-I2

Peers: Volex and Huber+Suhner (designed-in data-centre component suppliers whose narratives the sector digest compares with AQ); Halma (the market's discount on its single-customer data-centre unit); Kitron, Scanfil, Note, HANZA and Incap (Nordic contract manufacturers covered by the same sell-side teams); Hammond Power Solutions and Tamura (transformer makers discussed in the US data-centre transformer "super-cycle"); ABB (AQ's gatekeeper customer); Lifco and Addtech (Swedish serial acquirers whose capital allocation investors compare with AQ's). Operating conditions: a risk-off market in October 2026 in which AI-infrastructure equities are sold on financing concerns; rotation from premium compounders to cheaper cyclicals in a short-cycle turn; the 800V DC transition debate; the European truck cycle; rising bond yields. Inputs: How widely and by whom (DNB Carnegie, Nordea, Pareto, ABG Sundal Collier, Inderes, Redeye and others; investor commentary on X/Twitter, Nordic investor forums and newsletters) are these dynamics discussed for AQ Group: how much of Q3 2026's 17.9 percent organic growth came from the base business rather than data centres; whether AQ or ABB captures pricing in medium-voltage data-centre transformers; North American competitor capacity arriving from late 2027 (Hammond's Fort Worth site, Tamura's Tijuana expansion and EMG acquisition); the 800V DC and solid-state transformer re-award risk and AQ's prototypes; whether the Pillar II tax step is a 2027 income-statement event or mainly a cash event; working-capital absorption and cash conversion during the ramp; the SEK 22m Gävle property gain inside 2026 profit; delivery precision at 91.1 percent; customer concentration in data centres; the unused net-cash balance sheet and whether the 2031 EPS-doubling ambition depends on larger acquisitions; the Halma Avo precedent for valuing single-customer data-centre revenue; founder share ownership and the absence of buybacks? For each: which analysts and commentators raise it, how prominently, since when, with what view, and which dynamics no one discusses? Focus: Which of these dynamics for AQ Group are widely discussed by the sell-side and investors, which by only one or two, and which by no one, for the next 18 months and for 18 months to three years out, with particular attention to the less visible ones (the composition of 2026 growth, who captures data-centre pricing, the timing of the tax step, and how the 2031 EPS ambition is to be reached).

Request: OWN-1

Question: For AQ Group (AQ-SE), how much additional tax will the OECD Pillar Two global minimum tax impose on its profits in Bulgaria, Estonia and Lithuania (named in its 2025 annual report as failing the transitional safe harbours), has the company been accruing top-up tax in its income statement since Pillar Two began applying to it on 1 January 2025 or will the effect appear only when cash is first paid in 2027, how large are the substance-based income exclusions for its payroll and tangible assets in those countries and how fast do they decline, have those countries adopted qualified domestic minimum top-up taxes or other safe harbours (such as Bulgaria's 2025 amendments), and what effective and cash tax rates do brokers model and comparable Nordic manufacturers with Baltic or Bulgarian plants (Kitron, HANZA, Scanfil) report?

Request: OWN-2

Question: How does ABB source the transformers, inductors and enclosures in its HiPerGuard medium-voltage UPS and related data-centre power products, what share of that supply does AQ Group (AQ-SE) hold, does ABB dual-source or plan to insource these magnetics, how have prices and contract terms between ABB and its magnetics suppliers moved in 2025-26 while North American transformer makers raised prices, and what will ABB's 800V DC and solid-state transformer products (expected from the second half of 2027) mean for suppliers of conventional medium-voltage magnetics such as AQ Group?

Request: OWN-3

Question: What dry-type and medium-voltage transformer and data-centre magnetics capacity is being added in North America between 2026 and 2029 (Hammond Power Solutions' Fort Worth facility, Tamura's Tijuana expansion and EMG Power Electric acquisition, MGM Transformer, Forgent Power Solutions, Eaton, Schneider Electric, Siemens and others), how does it compare with forecast data-centre demand, what are lead times and prices doing, and what does the balance imply for the utilisation and pricing of AQ Group's (AQ-SE) Virginia and Cleveland (Norlake) transformer operations in 2027-29?

Request: OWN-4

Question: What are the 2027 production schedules, order intake and inventory positions at the European and North American heavy-truck, bus, construction-equipment, agricultural-machinery and rail makers that AQ Group (AQ-SE) supplies (Volvo Group, Scania and Traton, Daimler Truck, Volvo Construction Equipment, CNH, AGCO, Alstom, Hitachi Rail), how far has industrial and electronics restocking run, and what do Nordic contract manufacturers (Kitron, Scanfil, Note, HANZA, Inission) report about 2027 demand from these customers, so as to judge how AQ's base business will grow after its strong 2026 recovery?

Request: OWN-5

Question: Why did AQ Group's (AQ-SE) net working capital and inventory rise in 2026 (company Q2 and Q3 2026 reports: inventory turns 2.9 against a 3.5 target; Q2 operating cash flow SEK 23m), how much reflects inventory for data-centre and defence deliveries, receivables from late-quarter invoicing and acquired businesses, what payment terms, prepayments and inventory obligations do data-centre and defence customers impose on comparable suppliers (Kitron's customer deposits, Volex's hub-delivery inventory, Scanfil's framework agreements), and how quickly did AQ release working capital after its 2021-22 inventory build?

Request: OWN-6

Question: Is AQ Group (AQ-SE) likely to change its capital allocation in 2027-29 (larger or more frequent acquisitions, use of leverage, special dividends or a first share buyback) given its net cash, its ambition to double earnings per share again by 2031, its Large Cap listing since January 2026, its new independent chair Åsa Landén Ericsson and its founders' roughly one-third ownership; what have management, the board and the founders said; what is the pipeline and seller-multiple environment for small European, Indian and North American contract manufacturers and magnetics makers; and how were comparable Nordic compounders (Lifco, Addtech, Lagercrantz, HANZA) and Volex valued when they stepped up capital deployment?

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months
and Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

10 questions in 4 groups · 17,732 words · written 26 October 2026, 07:53 to 08:15 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
AQ Group (AQ-SE): answers to the third and fourth sets of questions

Written 2026-10-26 for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo. Money in SEK; "calculated" marks a figure I derived from the files rather than one stated in them. The forecasts of record are the Financial Analyst's model after update 1: revenue growth 12.8, 10.0, 8.0, 7.5 and 7.5 percent for FY2026-30; EBITA SEK 1,019m, 1,137m, 1,254m, 1,355m, 1,465m; EPS SEK 8.86, 9.78, 10.77, 11.67, 12.61. They sit 1.0 percent above consensus EPS for FY2026 and 3.7 and 2.5 percent below for FY2027 and FY2028 (consensus SEK 8.77, 10.15, 11.05; four brokers, all Hold). Three framing matters come first, because they change how the earlier sets should be read.

1. The free-cash-flow gap flagged in the model summary (FY2026: the model's SEK 274.6m against the Analyst's cross-check of SEK 495m) is a definitional and base-year problem, not a disagreement about the business. It understates the model's FY2026 cash generation by about SEK 150m, and I change the working-capital input to correct it. The model computes operating cash flow as EBITDA less the P&L tax charge less net financial items less the change in working capital, where working capital is FactSet's three-line balance (receivables plus inventories less payables) at the input percentage of revenue. Reconstructing the model (calculated): 31.0 percent of SEK 10,232m is SEK 3,172m, against FactSet's FY2025 year-end balance of SEK 2,657m (29.3 percent of revenue), an absorption of SEK 515m; EBITDA 1,371 less tax at 19.8 percent of pre-tax 1,013 (SEK 201m) less net financial items of SEK 6m less 515 gives operating cash flow of SEK 649m; less capex 280 and lease payments 95 gives SEK 274m, which is the model's figure to within a million. The Analyst's 495 rests on operating cash flow of about SEK 871m, which embeds an implicit absorption of about SEK 344m and a cash tax of about SEK 150m rather than the P&L charge. Two facts make the model's figure wrong for FY2026. First, nine months of the year are already reported: operating cash flow was SEK 631m, so the model's full-year 649 implies a fourth quarter of SEK 18m, which no plausible fourth quarter produces (Q4 2025 was SEK 123m on a smaller business; consensus implies about SEK 227m). Second, FactSet's three-line balance rose SEK 474m in those nine months while the company's cash-flow statement absorbed far less, for two reasons: about SEK 80 to 100m of the rise was acquired with Time 24 (SEK 37m of inventory plus receivables) and Norlake (SEK 9m of inventory) and is paid for through the acquisitions line, so the model counts it twice; and other operating liabilities (accruals, customer advances, provisions) grow with revenue and sit outside the three-line measure. The model's percentage therefore has to be calibrated to the model's mechanics rather than read as the balance-sheet ratio. On FactSet's definition I still expect about 31 percent at end-2026 and about 30 percent by 2029, as the second set did; but the input that reproduces the cash flow I believe is about 1.5 points lower in every year. I lower the working-capital line to 29.5, 29.3, 28.8, 28.5 and 28.5 percent (the section 6 file), keeping the Analyst's year-on-year profile intact. The effect (calculated): FY2026 operating cash flow of about SEK 800m (a fourth quarter of about SEK 170m, above Q4 2025 but below consensus because catch-up deliveries land late in the quarter and build receivables), free cash flow of about SEK 430m (model 275; Analyst 495); FY2027-30 free cash flow of roughly SEK 565m, 750m, 825m and 855m against the model's 551, 739, 812 and 841 and the Analyst's 620, 835, 915 and 955; year-end net cash including leases of about SEK 0.5bn at end-2026 and about SEK 1.85bn by 2030. I deliberately land below the Analyst's cross-check from FY2027 onward: the Analyst's cash figures embed only about 20 percent of incremental revenue absorbed in working capital, which presumes an inventory-turn improvement the company has not delivered since the 3.5 target was set; I carry about 27 percent in 2027 and 22 to 25 percent thereafter. Two smaller points for the Financial Analyst. The model uses the P&L tax charge as cash tax, which overstates FY2026 cash tax by about SEK 50m (Pillar II top-up has been accrued since 2025 but is first paid in 2027) and understates FY2027 cash tax by SEK 20 to 40m: roughly a wash across the two years, so I do not change the tax line for it. And the summary's phasing note (the Q4 2026 period margin moved up 1.6 points to match the year) is a basis artefact: the model's quarterly history is FactSet's EBITDA, which understates the company's by SEK 10 to 19m a quarter, so the model's standalone Q4 2026 must not be used to judge the 11 February 2027 print. Judge that print against company-basis revenue of about SEK 2,730m (plus 17.5 percent) and EBIT of about SEK 270m (9.9 percent).

2. The SEK 22m Gävle property gain sits in 2025, not 2026, and in net financial items, not EBIT. Both earlier sets have this wrong, and the correction removes a bearish premise from the second set. The first set (C-15, C-54) placed the gain in 2026 and the second set built a framing point on it ("the 2026 base is flattered by about 0.2 points"; an underlying FY2026 EBIT margin of 9.65 percent; FY2026 EPS "including about SEK 0.19" of gain). Two of the AlphaSense reports written for this work, citing the 2025 annual report of 25 March 2026 and the Q3 2026 interim report, state that the gain was realised in April 2025 on the divestment of the property subsidiary, was recorded within net financial items, and lifted the Q2 2025 EBT margin by about one point; DNB Carnegie adjusted for it at the time. The financials block corroborates this independently: Q2 2025 reported net income of SEK 188m on EBIT of SEK 202m is a 93 percent ratio against about 80 percent in every neighbouring quarter, which requires roughly SEK 25m of non-operating income in that quarter; FactSet's FY2025 pre-tax profit (SEK 800m) sits below the company's SEK 831m by about the gain plus items FactSet classes as unusual; and the "SEK 2.06" prior-year EPS that the first set flagged as disclosure noise is the Q2 2025 figure that contained the gain. Consequences: (i) the 2026 EBIT base is clean, so the nine-month 2026 EBIT margin of 9.85 percent is the underlying run-rate, not 9.6 percent, and the model's FY2027 EBIT margin of 10.0 percent is a step of only about 0.15 points from that run-rate, more conservative than the second set believed; I therefore leave the margin inputs unchanged, with more confidence in them; (ii) the FY2025 base was the flattered one: underlying FY2025 EBT margin was about 8.9 percent rather than 9.2, and underlying FY2025 EPS about SEK 7.2 to 7.3 rather than the brokers' SEK 7.46, so underlying FY2026 EPS growth is about 22 percent, not 18 (calculated); (iii) the second set's "incremental EBIT margin of 13.5 percent ex gain" for FY2027 is really the reported 11.3 percent, which is the right figure to carry and is consistent with acquired dilution and step-fixed capacity costs.

3. Where I disagree with the earlier sets I say so in the answers. The main disagreements are the two above; the second set's optimism on cash conversion from FY2027; and the first set's claim that Q3 2026 was struck against a trough comparison (Q3 2025 organic growth was plus 4.6 percent, so the base business's roughly 12 percent organic growth in Q3 2026 was a stronger and less comparison-driven signal than the first set allowed, which matters for how fast the base decelerates in 2027).

The third set: assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Risks

C577 questions
D-0166D-0264D-0352D-0455D-0550D-0656D-0758

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The financial forecasts for the next 18 months are more predictable than those of almost any contract manufacturer and somewhat less predictable than the company's thirty-year record suggests, because the shape of the next 18 months is a scheduled deceleration from an exceptional quarter, and decelerations are where forecasts and share prices part company. The earlier sets cohere on every mechanism that matters: a clean 10 percent margin reached in 2026 and held (not raised) through 2027, a structural slice growing at the pace of its own capacity, a base business recovering then flattening, tax rising under Pillar II, cash lagging growth and releasing when growth slows, and a balance sheet left idle by habit. The two factual errors I found (the Gävle gain's timing and the model's working-capital calibration) both resolve in the forecasts' favour: the 2026 base is cleaner than the second set thought, and the model's FY2026 cash was too low by about SEK 150m. The forecasts rely, for the window, on strengths AQ has demonstrated repeatedly, with one exception (a full year above 10 percent) that the model does not actually require until FY2028.

What limits predictability is concentrated in three places. First, the base business: it supplied most of Q3 2026's growth on share gains and North American utilisation rather than easy comparisons, and whether it grows 3 percent or minus 4 percent in the second half of 2027 is worth 8 to 10 percent of FY2027 EBIT either way; the OEM evidence favours the first half and threatens the second. Second, the common factor: hyperscaler capital spending and European industrial demand both run on financing conditions that are tightening, so the bear case is one shock, not two, which skews the operating cone down (EPS minus 15 to plus 10 percent for FY2027, probability-weighted about 2 percent below the model and 5 percent below consensus). Third, the share price: 78 percent of AQ's variance is its own, so the multiple will follow the company's growth rate down from mid-2027 even if EBIT lands on forecast, and the remaining 22 percent is the AI-infrastructure trade that the market is currently selling. Disclosure is candid and lets the Consumer watch growth, execution and cash each quarter but never the engine's margin, so the FY2026 and Q1 2027 group margins are the tests; the external chain (ABB's orders, truck OEM schedules, copper, the krona, the tax note) is unusually legible and leads AQ's numbers by one to two quarters.

For the Consumer: carry the model's EBIT with high weight through Q1 2027 and medium weight thereafter; carry cash with the calibrated inputs and expect single-year misses; treat the organic-growth alarm at 10 percent as scheduled for mid-2027 rather than as a thesis break; and treat the multiple's cone (15.5 to 23 times) as wider than the earnings cone, which is the main reason the predictability of the forecasts does not translate into predictability of the return.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

C66

Judgement: the two sets are highly congruent on the architecture of the case and on every conclusion that drives the forecasts; they contradict each other, or the evidence, on a handful of facts and on the direction of two inferences, and none of those contradictions overturns the base case. The incongruities cluster exactly where the forecasts are least predictable (the base business's path through the second half of 2027, and working capital), which is itself useful information.

What aligns, and why it matters for predictability. Seven conclusions run through both sets without contradiction and each is carried into the model: (1) AQ is two businesses in one set of accounts, a cyclical base of about 86 percent of sales earning 9 to 9.5 percent EBIT and a structural slice of about 14 percent (data-centre magnetics, defence, grid) growing 30 to 60 percent at an estimated 13 to 14 percent margin; the model is built as that overlay. (2) The margin is capped near 10 percent by industry structure (price-taking sub-supplier, OEM productivity cuts of 3 to 5 percent a year, no intellectual property) and lifted only by mix and site fixes; the model's EBIT margin creeps from 9.9 to 10.3 percent over five years. (3) Cash conversion is the weak link because net working capital runs at about 30 percent of sales; both sets expect conversion well below 1.0 while growth exceeds 8 to 10 percent. (4) Tax turns from a tailwind (14 to 16 percent in 2020-23) into a headwind (20 percent today, 21 to 22 percent by 2028-30) under Pillar II, and consensus's implied fall to 19.7 percent in FY2027 is the least defensible line in consensus. (5) The growth target of 15 percent is aspiration, not forecast; the margin target above 10 percent is a normal-conditions floor. (6) The balance sheet is an unused lever whose use is the only route to the 2031 EPS-doubling ambition, and nothing in management's record puts it in the base case. (7) Share-price risk exceeds earnings risk, because the premium multiple rests on an AI-power narrative that the market is currently selling on its financing. Because every one of these is an independent observation that points the same way (growth decelerating gracefully, margin flat-to-up, EPS compounding at about 10 to 11 percent, cash improving as growth slows), the model's base case is not the product of one view but of a consistent reading, which raises the weight it deserves.

What jars, with my resolution of each.

  • The Gävle gain (both sets wrong; resolved above). A factual error propagated through both sets and into the second set's framing. Its correction changes the direction of one inference (the 2026 base is clean; the 2025 base was flattered) but not the conclusion (margin near 10 percent in 2027), because the second set had set its 2027 margin against a 9.65 percent "underlying" base that was in fact 9.85 percent, so the 2027 step it modelled is smaller and safer than it intended.
  • The free-cash-flow gap (resolved above). The second set's inputs and its own cross-check did not reconcile because the input is a balance-sheet ratio and the model treats it as a cash driver from a FactSet base year that excludes other operating liabilities and includes acquired balances. A calibration problem, not a disagreement about the business; the second set's cross-check is itself somewhat optimistic from 2027.
  • The weight of metal pass-through (first set C-05 and C-53 against second set P-04). The first set treated the copper and steel lag as a material margin item and as the explanation for consensus's fall in value-added gross margin; the second set showed from the 2025 annual report that metal content in purchases is about 2.4 percent of sales, so a 10 percent copper move is about 0.2 percent of sales and the gross-margin decline is mix (material-heavy transformers, defence mechanics, bought-in systems). The AlphaSense reports confirm AQ buys semi-finished goods and that the lag is three to six months. The second set is right; the first set's margin-risk framing should be read as a 0.2 to 0.4 point, one-to-two-quarter item, not a thesis risk. Congruent after correction.
  • What drove Q3 2026 (first set C-01 and C-08 against second set framing point 4, and against the AlphaSense evidence). The first set attributed the acceleration mainly to data centres and easy comparisons; the second set computed that data centres (about 7 percent of sales growing above 60 percent) added about 2.6 points and defence about 1, leaving the base at roughly 12 to 16 percent organic. The sell-side narrative report records management's explanation that growth came from new e-truck nominations, share gains and full utilisation in the US and China, with European industrial markets still sluggish, and the driver-concentration report records Q3 2025 organic growth of plus 4.6 percent, not a trough. So the base business grew about 12 percent organically on a non-trough comparison. The second set's conclusion (the 2027 deceleration is a base-business question first) stands and is strengthened; the first set's "easy comparison" explanation is weakened. The residual tension is that a base growing 12 percent on share gains and US utilisation is harder to decelerate to plus 3 percent for 2027 than a base growing on restocking; this is the single most important unresolved question for the 18-month revenue line and I return to it in D-03, D-04 and I-02.
  • Downside operating leverage (first set C-75 against second set P-01). The first set described decremental margins as historically near zero (2020, 2024); the second set argued the next downturn would be worse (15 to 20 percent decremental) because the 2024 cushion was metal deflation and a workforce cut of about 1,000 temporary staff, and because the structural slice now carries step-fixed capacity. The AlphaSense peer evidence (Kitron held a 7.4 percent EBIT margin on a 16.5 percent revenue fall in 2024 by cutting 20 percent of staff; Comer held a 16.7 percent EBITDA margin on a 23 percent fall by cutting direct labour 18.6 percent; Inission's margin fell to 5.4 percent when cuts lagged) supports the second set's view that resilience is a management action with a lag, not a structural property. Congruent after revision; the 8 percent EBIT floor both sets name is the right deep-downturn anchor.
  • Skew (second set). The second set says the FY2027 EPS cone is skewed down (bear minus 15 percent, bull plus 9 percent) while also saying the balance sheet and unannounced acquisitions are the main source of upside and sit outside all three scenarios. These statements are compatible but must be read together: the operating cone is skewed down because the two engines share a cause (financing conditions), while a separate, management-controlled upside (capital deployment) lies outside it. For predictability, the operating skew is what matters; for the fourth set, the outside-the-cone upside is what consensus omits.
  • The fair multiple (first set C-50 against the model's guideline). The first set judged a fair 24-month-forward P/E of 18 to 21 times in normal conditions; the model's guideline fair multiple is 22.8 times and the 18-month total shareholder return of plus 21.7 percent is computed at it. This is not a forecast incongruity but the Consumer should know that the return in the one-page summary rests on a multiple one notch above the first set's own view (topic #11 is the Consumer's call; at 20 times the 18-month return would be roughly plus 8 percent including dividends, calculated).
  • Customer concentration in data centres. The first set carried "one customer about 50 percent of data-centre sales" from the Q2 2026 call; the Q3 2026 call said sales are now spread across several customers; the ABB sourcing report puts ABB at 9 percent of group sales (second-largest customer) and "up to 10 percent" of revenue across programmes. These are consistent with a concentration that is falling from a high level, and the model's data-centre line does not depend on which reading is right over 18 months.
  • The base business in 2027 (second set against the OEM-schedules report). The second set models the base at plus 3 percent organic for 2027, implying slight declines in the second half; the OEM report shows Volvo's 2027 outlook of 320,000 European heavy trucks (plus 3 percent) and 300,000 North American Class 8 (plus 13 percent), Volvo order intake up 26 percent, Daimler North American deliveries up 51 percent, Volvo Construction Equipment orders up 16 percent and the US short-cycle index 62 percent above 2019, against dealer inventories of 3.3 months, September Class 8 orders down 7 percent, agriculture at the cycle bottom and European short-cycle demand still below its 2021 peak. The evidence is more mixed than "flat to fragile": it supports a first half of 2027 above the second set's path and a second half that depends on North American destocking and European sentiment. I keep the full-year inputs but regard the quarterly phasing (plus 16.2 percent in Q1, plus 6.8 percent in Q3) as having an upside lean in the first half and a downside lean in the second (D-04).

The judgement that follows. Congruence is high where it needs to be: the mechanisms that make the model's EPS sit 3 to 4 percent below consensus in FY2027-28 (tax, a small margin step, no unannounced acquisitions, slightly lower revenue) are the same mechanisms in both sets and in the AlphaSense evidence. The contradictions are in facts that have now been corrected and in two inferences whose correction leaves the base case intact or slightly stronger (the margin base is clean; the base business is stronger than "easy comparisons"). The places where the sets do not fully join (the base's second-half 2027 path; the pace of working-capital release) are the places where I would put the widest error bars, and D-04 does so.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

C64

Judgement: for the next 18 months the expected change is highly plausible and rests about four-fifths on demonstrated strengths; the undemonstrated fifth is a full year of EBT above 10 percent (never printed; the record is quarterly) and the conversion of the Norlake and 2027 capacity steps into revenue at group margins. Beyond 18 months the forecast quietly depends on three strengths the business does not yet have (North American scale in medium-voltage magnetics, a design-in on solid-state or 800V platforms, and a bench of site leaders able to run several turnarounds at once), and the model's 2028-30 path assumes partial acquisition of the first and third.

The expected change, stated precisely. Against 2019-25 (revenue plus 10 percent a year, EBIT margin from 6.4 to 9.3 percent, EPS plus 17 percent a year, cash conversion 83 percent on FactSet's loose basis), the model expects revenue plus 9 percent a year, an EBIT margin held at 10.0 to 10.3 percent, EPS plus 10 to 11 percent a year and cash conversion rising toward 0.8 as growth slows. The "change" is therefore less a step-up than the holding of a level reached in 2026 while growth decelerates. The components that must be delivered: a data-centre magnetics line growing from about SEK 670m to about SEK 1,000m in 2027 (plus 50 percent) and SEK 1,200m in 2028; defence plus 25 then plus 15 percent; a base business growing about 3 percent organically; acquired growth of about 3 percent a year; an EBIT margin of 10.0 percent in 2027 and 10.2 percent in 2028; a tax rate near 21 percent.

Strengths demonstrably held, with the evidence that proves them.

  • Profit protection through cycles: positive net profit in every quarter since 1994 (117 quarters at the last count in the files); EBT above 8 percent for 14 consecutive quarters; EBIT margin up from 8.8 to 9.6 percent in the 2024 downturn on organic revenue down 6.9 percent, achieved by cutting about 1,000 mainly temporary staff; a record operating cash flow above SEK 1bn in that downturn. This is the strength the 10 percent margin floor relies on, and it is the best-evidenced thing in the case.
  • The margin level itself at the quarterly scale: EBT 9.9 percent in Q2 2026 and 10.0 percent in Q3 2026, EBIT 10.1 percent in Q3, on a clean base (the Gävle correction above), against a seasonally weak third quarter. Volex's underlying operating margin of 10.2 percent in its FY2026 as data-centre sales doubled, and Kitron's 9.5 percent in Q3 2026 on defence, show that about 10 percent is attainable for contract manufacturers in this mix; DNB Carnegie's own view in May 2025 that above 10 percent was unsustainable has been overtaken by two quarters of delivery.
  • Volume execution in the growth niche: medium-voltage transformer output doubled sequentially in Q2 2026 to 70 systems a month; data-centre inductive deliveries up more than 60 percent in Q3; Virginia full at about USD 50m of sales; "winning every US quote" (DNB Carnegie). Demand is confirmed one tier up: ABB's electrification orders of about USD 7.7bn in Q3 2026, up 70 percent organically, with data-centre orders up triple digits and a book-to-bill of 1.41.
  • Turnaround and integration: mdexx from minus 7 to plus 7 percent EBT and at group margins by Q3 2026, about 18 months after purchase; Riedel stabilised; Trafotek and Schaffner Power Magnetics integrated (the latter took two to three years to reach group profitability, per DNB Carnegie). This is the strength the acquired-growth line and the Norlake fill rely on.
  • Acquisition discipline: no deals in 2025 when multiples exceeded criteria; three cheap deals in 2026 (Time 24 for GBP 1 plus debt; Gumboda SEK 2m; Norlake at USD 13.5m enterprise value, mostly property and liabilities). Historic entry multiple about 6 times EBITA while Nordic serial acquirers pay 6 to 8 times and European EMS transactions have moved from 11 to 15 times EV/EBIT (Nordea).
  • Cash release when growth stops: inventory from SEK 816m (2020) to SEK 1,629m (2022), turns at a record low 2.3; then a dedicated reduction programme, turns back to 3.2 by end-2023 and operating cash flow of SEK 980m in 2023 from SEK 208m in 2022. This demonstrates the self-hedging balance sheet the second set relies on in its downside case, within about four quarters.
  • Cost flexibility in the base: about 65 percent of staff in Bulgaria, Poland, Lithuania and Estonia; 20 percent annual turnover; hours and headcount adjusted locally within weeks (Q1 2024 call).

Strengths the forecast needs that are not yet demonstrated, and how much of the forecast rests on each.

  • A full fiscal year of EBT above 10 percent. No full year has exceeded 9.6 percent (FY2024); FY2025 was about 8.9 percent underlying. The model has FY2026 at 9.8 percent and FY2027 at 9.96 percent, so it does not actually require the target to be met for the 18-month EPS path; it requires the quarterly level of mid-2026 to hold on average through 2027 while acquired units dilute by about 0.1 point and new capacity carries start-up cost. Plausible on the record (three consecutive quarters at 9.9 to 10.1 percent EBIT, with Q3 flattered by perhaps 0.2 to 0.3 points of temporary US and China utilisation). Dependence: high for EPS; moderate risk.
  • Filling Norlake and the 2027 transformer lines. Norlake had USD 7m of 2025 sales (SEK 69m) and below-average profitability; management cites up to USD 35m at full utilisation; the model's data-centre line of SEK 1,000m in 2027 and SEK 1,200m in 2028 needs US inductive deliveries roughly to double over two to three years, which is what the company has said.
